Weak Waldhausen categories and a localization theorem
Yasuaki Ogawa, Amit Shah

Abstract
. Waldhausen categories were introduced to extend algebraic K K -theory beyond Quillen’s exact categories. In this article, we modify Waldhausen’s axioms so that it matches better with the theory of extriangulated categories, introducing a weak Waldhausen category and defining its Grothendieck group. Examples of weak Waldhausen categories include any extriangulated category, hence any exact or triangulated category, and any Waldhausen category. A key feature of this structure is that it allows for “one-sided” extriangulated localization theory, and thus enables us to extract right exact sequences of Grothendieck groups that we cannot obtain from the theory currently available. To demonstrate the utility of our Weak Waldhausen Localization Theorem, we give three applications. First, we give a new proof of the Extriangulated Localization Theorem proven by Enomoto–Saito, which is a generalization at the level of K 0 K_{0} of Quillen’s classical Localization Theorem for exact categories. Second, we give a new proof that the index with respect to an n n -cluster tilting subcategory 𝒳 \mathscr{X} of a triangulated category 𝒞 \mathscr{C} induces an isomorphism between K 0 𝗌𝗉 ​ ( 𝒳 ) K_{0}^{\mathsf{sp}}(\mathscr{X}) and the Grothendieck group of an extriangulated substructure of 𝒞 \mathscr{C} . Last, we produce a weak Waldhausen K 0 K_{0} -generalization of a localization construction due to Sarazola that involves cotorsion pairs but allows for non-Serre localizations. We show that the right exact sequences of Grothendieck groups obtained from our Sarazola construction and the Extriangulated Localization Theorem agree under a common setup.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 18F25; Secondary 18E05, 18E35, 18G50, 18G80

1.
Introduction

The idea behind
K
-theory is to assign invariants, known as
K
-groups, to an object
X
of interest, e.g. a ring, topological space, category etc.
Although this purpose of
K
-groups is modestly stated, they can (if well-defined) reflect key properties of
X
and in doing so have been used to answer significant questions. Indeed,
K
-theory has a broad spectrum of connections to other fields, including algebraic geometry, topology, number theory, ring theory, and string theory (see e.g.
[
Wei99
]
,
[
FG05
, Preface]
,
[
Wei13
, p. x]
).

The
K
-theory of a ring
R
begins with its Grothendieck group
K_{0}(R)
(due to Grothendieck
[
SGA6
]
), the Whitehead group
K_{1}(R)
(due to Bass–Schanuel
[
BS62
]
), and the group
K_{2}(R)
(due to Milnor
[
Mil71
]
), all defined using linear algebra and basic group theory.
However, it turned out that producing a suitable notion of
K_{i}(R)
for
i\geq 3
using a similar approach was somewhat harder to see at that time.

This was impressively overcome by Quillen through his
+
-construction for rings
[
Qui71
]
. Firstly, his work used geometric and topological methods, and secondly he produced higher
K
-groups
K_{i}(R)
(
i\geq 0
) not one at a time, but rather all at once as the homotopy groups of a classifying space. In further groundbreaking work
[
Qui73
]
, Quillen laid out his
Q
-construction, which resulted in a unified and widely accepted higher algebraic
K
-theory for exact categories.
This was the beginning of higher algebraic
K
-theory and Quillen’s efforts in this direction earned him the Fields Medal in
1978
.

However, there was one drawback: computation in algebraic
K
-theory was generally challenging.
To mitigate this, Quillen gave several reduction results
[
Qui73
, Thms. 3, 4, 5]
, allowing one to determine the
K
-theory of more complicated categories via simpler ones.
The Localization Theorem
[
Qui73
, Thm. 5]
is one of the most powerful. It yields a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(\mathscr{A}/\mathscr{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{A}/\mathscr{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of
K
-groups from an exact sequence
\mathscr{B}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{A}/\mathscr{B}
of abelian categories (see Definition
4.10
), where
\mathscr{B}
is a Serre subcategory of
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{A}/\mathscr{B}
is the Serre quotient (i.e. a certain localization of
\mathscr{A}
).
This extended the right exact sequence of only Grothendieck groups given by Bass
[
Bas68
, Ch. VIII, Cor. 5.5]
(see also
[
Hel65
]
) to the left using higher
K
-groups.
A generalization of Quillen’s result for exact categories was proven by Cárdenas-Escudero
[
CE98
, Thm. 7.0.63]
.

Despite the wide scope of Quillen’s
K
-theory, it was not sufficiently general for some applications
K
-theorists had in mind, e.g. where triangulated categories come into play.
To tackle this problem, Waldhausen
[
Wal85
]
introduced a generalization of exact categories, now called Waldhausen categories (see Definition
2.1
). Waldhausen even gave non-additive examples arising from topology
[
Wei13
, Exam. II.9.1.4]
.
More importantly, it was shown that an algebraic
K
-theory still exists for Waldhausen categories and a Localization Theorem
[
Wal85
, Thm. 1.6.4]
was also proved.

As a consequence of Waldhausen’s theory,
Thomason–Trobaugh
[
TT90
]
established a
K
-theory for derived categories (of complicial biWaldhausen categories), as well as a Localization Theorem for the
K
-theory of schemes that was a substantial improvement on previous results in the literature; and later, Schlichting produced a
K
-theory for algebraic triangulated categories
[
Sch06
]
.
In particular, in analogy with Quillen’s
[
Qui73
, Thm. 5]
recalled above, the exact sequence of triangulated categories associated to the Verdier quotient of an algebraic triangulated category yields a long exact sequence of
K
-groups.

In this paper, we bring together several of the above ideas from the modern perspective of Nakaoka–Palu’s extriangulated categories
[
NP19
]
(see Section
4
), which give a simultaneous generalization of exact and triangulated categories.
See Figure
1
below for a summary of the interactions we establish between these ideas.
A localization theory for extriangulated categories was developed in
[
NOS22
]
by Nakaoka–Ogawa–Sakai, which recovers classical localizations such as the Serre and Verdier quotients of abelian and triangulated categories, respectively (see Section
4.3
).
Our first main result (see Theorem
A
below) is motivated by Schlichting’s cylinder-free Localization Theorem
[
Sch06
, Thm. 11]
for Waldhausen categories.
However, in order to draw from the theories of Waldhausen and extriangulated categories at the same time, we introduce the notion of a
weak Waldhausen
(additive) category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
and define its Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.
Just like how a Waldhausen category is a category
\mathscr{C}
equipped with classes
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
of cofibrations and weak equivalences, the data of a weak Waldhausen category includes classes
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
of distinguished
3
-term sequences and weak equivalences; see Definition
2.1
and Definition
2.13
.
An (additive) Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
gives rise canonically to a weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
, where
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
is the class of cofibration sequences of
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
(see Proposition
2.23
).
The following Localization Theorem for weak Waldhausen categories summarises our Theorem
3.6
and Corollary
3.7
.

Theorem A
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
and
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
are weak Waldhausen categories with
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
, and let
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})
be the weak Waldhausen subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic objects.
If

(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})\to(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})\to(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})

is a localization sequence (see Definition
2.22
)
of weak (bi)Waldhausen categories,
such that any weak equivalence in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
admits a suitable factorization,
then we have an exact sequence of their Grothendieck groups as follows.

(1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

As a first application of Theorem
A
, we prove that
an extriangulated localization sequence (see Theorem
4.14
and Remark
4.15
(1)
) naturally gives rise to a localization sequence of weak Waldhausen categories which induces a right exact sequence (
1.1
).
Consequently, we recover and, through the lens of weak Waldhausen categories, give a new proof of Enomoto–Saito’s
[
ES22
, Cor. 4.32]
, which is a Localization Theorem for extriangulated categories; see Corollary
5.14
.

An important aspect of the viewpoint we adopt here is that weak Waldhausen categories allow us to consider “one-sided” localizations that are beyond the scope of the extriangulated localization theory of
[
NOS22
]
. This viewpoint was taken in
[
Oga22a
,
Oga22b
]
, unifying several important constructions in the literature e.g.
[
BBD82
,
KZ08
,
KR07
,
Bel13
,
BM12
,
BM13
]
. In Section
6
,
we prove Theorem
6.14
and exploit this one-sided localization perspective to understand the index in triangulated categories.

The index with respect to a
2
-cluster tilting subcategory of a triangulated category
\mathscr{C}
was introduced by Palu
[
Pal08
]
, and this definition was generalized to
n
-cluster tilting subcategories
\mathscr{X}
in
[
Jør21
]
for
n\geq 2
an integer. The importance of the index is evidenced, for instance, by its use in the theory of
cluster algebras
[
Pal08
,
DK08
,
Pal09
,
Pla11
]
and of
friezes
[
Guo13
,
HJ15
,
HJ16
,
Jør21
,
JS24a
]
.
The index with respect to
\mathscr{X}
induces an isomorphism between the split Grothendieck group

K^{\mathsf{sp}}_{0}(\mathscr{X})
and the Grothendieck group
of a certain extriangulated substructure of the triangulated structure on
\mathscr{C}
; see Corollary
6.6
or
[
OS23
, Cor. 5.5]
, and also
[
JS24b
, Thm. B]
,
[
PPPP23
, Prop. 4.11]
.
Using Theorem
6.14
, and hence as a second application of Theorem
A
, we give a proof of this index isomorphism using some reduction-style techniques; see Section
6.3
.
In fact, this process recovers the Extriangulated Resolution Theorem
[
OS23
, Thm. 4.5]
, demonstrating that there is a close connection between the Localization Theorem and the Resolution Theorem, two fundamental
K
-theoretic reduction theorems.

As our third and final application of Theorem
A
, we generalize to the extriangulated setting a construction due to Sarazola
[
Sar20
]
.
Sarazola
[
Sar20
, Thm. 3]
gives a Localization Theorem for exact categories allowing one to “localize” at subcategories that are not necessarily Serre, and this is done using Waldhausen categories and cotorsion pairs.
We produce a
K_{0}
-version of
[
Sar20
, Thm. 3]
for extriangulated categories and cotorsion pairs on them, using our weak Waldhausen categories; see Proposition
7.2
.

We will encounter exact sequences of extriangulated categories and Grothendieck groups, and localization sequences of (weak) Waldhausen categories in this article. We summarize
some key ideas and links we make in this article in Figure
1
:

of

1.1.
Notation and conventions

Throughout this article we adopt the following conventions.
For any category
\mathscr{C}
, we use the following notation.

•

\mathscr{C}(A,B)
is the class of morphisms
A\to B
for objects
A,B\in\mathscr{C}
.

•

\mathscr{C}^{\to}
is the class of all morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.

•

\mathscr{C}^{\to\to}
is the subclass of
\mathscr{C}^{\to}\times\mathscr{C}^{\to}
consisting of all pairs
(f,g)
of composable morphisms, indicated by diagrams

A\overset{f}{\to}B\overset{g}{\to}C
.

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\to}
is the class of all isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{isoclass}}(\mathscr{C})
is the class of all isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathscr{C}
.

Subcategories are usually full but not always (e.g. see Remark
2.4
), so we explicitly indicate this.
Functors are covariant unless stated otherwise.

Suppose
\mathscr{C}
is an additive category for the remainder of Section
1.1
.
If
f\colon A\to B
and
g\colon C\to D
are in
\mathscr{C}^{\to}
,
then we denote the unique induced morphism

A\oplus C\to B\oplus D
by

f\oplus g=\begin{pmatrix}f&0\\
0&g\end{pmatrix}
.
A functor between additive categories is always additive.
If
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
is an additive functor,
then its
kernel

\operatorname{Ker}F\subseteq\mathscr{C}

and

essential image

\operatorname{Im}F\subseteq\mathscr{D}

are the full subcategories defined as follows:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ker}F

\displaystyle=\Set{C\in\mathscr{C}}{FC\cong 0\textnormal{\ in\ }\mathscr{D}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}F

\displaystyle=\Set{D\in\mathscr{D}}{FC\cong D\textnormal{\ for some\ }C\in\mathscr{C}}.

By an
additive
subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, we mean a full subcategory
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
that is closed under finite direct sums.
If
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is an additive subcategory, then
[\mathscr{A}]
is the (two-sided) ideal of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
that factor through an object in
\mathscr{A}
, and the canonical additive quotient functor is
\overline{(-)}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/[\mathscr{A}]
. In this case,
the image in
\mathscr{C}/[\mathscr{A}]
of a morphism
f\in\mathscr{C}(A,B)
is denoted
\overline{f}
.

We denote the abelian category of abelian groups by
\mathsf{Ab}
.
Lastly, for reference, we list some categories of categories (and where they are introduced) that will often appear in the sequel:

•

\mathsf{Wald}
: the category of skeletally small Waldhausen (additive) categories (Section
2.1
);

•

\mathsf{wWald}
: the category of skeletally small weak Waldhausen (additive) categories (Section
2.2
); and

•

\mathsf{Extri}
: the category of skeletally small extriangulated categories (Section
4
).

2.
Weak Waldhausen categories

A Waldhausen category is a category equipped with cofibrations and weak equivalences
[
Wal85
]
. In this way, Waldhausen categories are a generalization of exact categories that still permit a higher
K
-theory. In Section
2.1
we recall the definitions and basic properties of a Waldhausen category.
The axioms for Waldhausen categories rely on the notion of a pushout. Since pushouts do not necessarily exist in an arbitrary extriangulated category, we introduce
weak Waldhausen additive
categories in Section
2.2
and show that any extriangulated category is indeed an example.
We introduce the Grothendieck group of a weak Waldhausen additive category in Section
2.3
, and this is used in Section
3
to produce a generalization of Quillen’s localization theorem.

2.1.
Classical Waldhausen categories

In this article we only consider a Waldhausen structure on an
additive
category
\mathscr{C}
. Waldhausen’s original definition in
[
Wal85
]
only required a pointed category, but nothing is lost for our purposes.
We refer to
[
Wei13
, Ch. II, Sec. 9]
for a succinct treatment of the basics of Waldhausen categories.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category (with a distinguished zero object
0
) equipped with two classes

\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
and

\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}

of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.
Morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
) are called
cofibrations
(resp.
weak equivalences
) and indicated with feathered arrows

(resp. decorated arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

).

The triplet
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a
Waldhausen (additive) category
if the following axioms are satisfied.

(C0)

The class
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
is closed under composition and contains each isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}
.

(C1)

The unique morphism
0\to C
to any object in
\mathscr{C}
is a cofibration.

(C2)

For any cofibration
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B
and any morphism
A\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}C
, there exists a pushout diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbin{\amalg}_{A}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(PO)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with the morphism
C\overset{g}{\rightarrowtail}B\mathbin{\amalg}_{A}C
being a cofibration.

(W0)

The class
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is closed under composition and contains each isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}
.

(W1)

(Gluing Axiom) Consider a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

in which all vertical arrows are weak equivalences and the feathered arrows are cofibrations.
Then the induced map
B_{1}\mathbin{\amalg}_{A_{1}}C_{1}\to B_{2}\mathbin{\amalg}_{A_{2}}C_{2}
is also a weak equivalence.

A Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is simply denoted by
\mathscr{C}
if there is no confusion.

Remark 2.2
.

(1)

Axiom
(C0)
has two immediate consequences. First, it implies
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
is closed under isomorphism.
Second, in the context of
(C2)
, any pushout of
f
along
c
is a cofibration. That is, if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(PO)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

is a pushout square in
\mathscr{C}
, then
g^{\prime}\colon C\rightarrowtail P
is also a cofibration.

(2)

Similarly, axiom
(W0)
also has some nice consequences. Indeed,
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is also closed under isomorphism; and, in the setup of
(W1)
, the canonical morphism from any pushout of
f_{1}
along
c_{1}
to any pushout of
f_{2}
along
c_{2}
is a weak equivalence (see Lemma
2.18
).

(3)

Let
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B
be a cofibration. Using
(C2)
, we see that
f
admits a cokernel in
\mathscr{C}
.
Note that any cokernel
g\colon B\to C
of
f
satisfies
C\cong 0\mathbin{\amalg}_{A}B
.
We call any cokernel sequence
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}C
(i.e.
g=\operatorname{cok}\nolimits f
) a
cofibration sequence
, and any cokernel
g
of
f
is called a
fibration
.
Note that the class of cofibration sequences is closed under isomorphisms, but that the class
\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}}
of fibrations is not closed under composition in general.

Example 2.3
.

Any exact category
(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E})
can be regarded as a Waldhausen category by declaring that all (admissible) inflations are cofibrations and all isomorphisms are weak equivalences.
In this case, the collection of all cofibration sequences is
\mathcal{E}
.

Remark 2.4
.

In the original language of
[
Wal85
]
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
are considered as subcategories of
\mathscr{C}
that are
not necessarily full
. For instance, from the class
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
of morphisms, we define a subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, denoted also by
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
by abuse of notation, with
\operatorname{Ob}(\operatorname{\mathsf{C}})=\operatorname{Ob}(\mathscr{C})
and with
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}(A,B)=\Set{f\in\mathscr{C}(A,B)}{f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}}
.
Note that
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
must contain all objects of
\mathscr{C}
as
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
contains all identity morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
.
Similarly, we can think of
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
as a subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.
However,
\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}
cannot be thought of as subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
since it is not necessarily closed under composition (see Remark
2.2
(3)
).

We will need the notion of a morphism of Waldhausen categories.

Definition 2.5
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
and
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
are Waldhausen categories.

(1)

An additive functor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is called
exact
if

F(\operatorname{\mathsf{C}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime}
,

F(\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
,
and, for every cofibration
f\colon A\rightarrowtail B
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
and every morphism
c\colon A\to C
in
\mathscr{C}
,
the canonical morphism

FB\mathbin{\amalg}_{FA}FC\to F(B\mathbin{\amalg}_{A}C)

is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
\mathscr{C}
is an additive subcategory of
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{inc}\colon\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is the inclusion functor, then
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is called a
Waldhausen subcategory
of
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
if:

•

\mathsf{inc}
is an exact functor of Waldhausen categories;

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}=\Set{f\colon A\rightarrowtail B}{\begin{aligned} &A,B\in\mathscr{C}\text{ and }f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime}\text{, and if }A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\twoheadrightarrow C\text{ is a}\\
&\text{cofibration sequence in }\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\text{, then }C\in\mathscr{C}\end{aligned}}
; and

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\to}=\Set{f\colon\hbox to55.81pt{\vbox to10.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 27.9066pt\lower-3.5165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.59898pt}{1.43611pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.98 1.99)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{A,B\in\mathscr{C}\text{ and }f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}
.

Note that the composition of exact functors is exact as well.
Thus, we denote by
\mathsf{Wald}
the category of skeletally small Waldhausen categories and exact functors.

The following additional axioms are standard in classical Waldhausen theory (see
[
Wal85
, p. 327]
) and will be used to define localization sequences in
\mathsf{Wald}
(see Definition
2.11
).

Definition 2.6
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
be a Waldhausen category.

(1)

Saturation axiom
:
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
satisfies the
2
-out-of-
3
property for compositions of morphisms; namely, for any composite morphism
gf
, if any two of
f,g,gf
belong to
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
, then so does the third.

(2)

Extension axiom
: For any commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

of cofibration sequences in
\mathscr{C}
, if
a,c\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
then
b\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.

For later use, we prove the following easy lemma.

Lemma 2.7
.

Any split exact sequence is a cofibration sequence in a Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.

Proof.

First, for any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
, note that if we pushout the cofibration
0\rightarrowtail Y
along
0\rightarrowtail X
, we see that the canonical inclusion
\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{id}_{X}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\colon X\to X\oplus Y(\cong Y\mathbin{\amalg}_{0}X)
is a cofibration.
Now suppose
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
is a split exact sequence.
Then
f
is isomorphic to
\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{id}_{A}\\
0\end{pmatrix}
by the Splitting Lemma
[
Sha20
, Lem. 2.7]
, so
f
is also a cofibration by Remark
2.2
(1)
.
Since
g=\operatorname{cok}\nolimits f
, we have that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
is a cofibration sequence by definition (see Remark
2.2
(3)
).
∎

2.1.1.
The Grothendieck group and some localization theorems

There is a natural definition of the Grothendieck group of a Waldhausen category, which extends to a functor
K_{0}\colon\mathsf{Wald}\to\mathsf{Ab}
.
For a skeletally small additive category
\mathscr{C}
, we denote the
split Grothendieck group of
\mathscr{C}
by
K^{\mathsf{sp}}_{0}(\mathscr{C})
.
Recall that
K^{\mathsf{sp}}_{0}(\mathscr{C})
is the free abelian group generated by symbols
[A]
, one for each
A\in\mathscr{C}
, modulo the subgroup generated by
elements
[A]-[B]+[C]
, one for each split exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C.}

Definition 2.8
.

Assume that
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a skeletally small Waldhausen category.
The
Grothendieck group

K_{0}(\mathscr{C})\coloneqq K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is

K^{\mathsf{sp}}_{0}(\mathscr{C})
modulo the relations:

•

[C]=[C^{\prime}]
for each weak equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
and

•

[B]=[A]+[C]
for each cofibration sequence
A\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow C
.

From Example
2.3
, it is clear that if
(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E})
is a skeletally small exact category and
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
the corresponding Waldhausen structure, then
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is the same as the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\mathscr{C})\coloneqq K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E})
of the exact category
(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E})
.

In the rest of this subsection, we motivate our main results by recalling two key localization theorems that are classical. The first concerns the localization of an abelian category by a Serre subcategory. We also remark that Quillen generalized Theorem
2.9
when introducing higher algebraic
K
-theory (see
[
Qui73
, Sec. 5, Thm. 5]
).

Theorem 2.9
.

[
Bas68
, Ch. VIII, Cor. 5.5]

Let
\mathscr{N}\to\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
be a Serre quotient of an abelian category
\mathscr{C}
by a Serre subcategory
\mathscr{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
.
Then it induces an exact sequence
K_{0}(\mathscr{N})\to K_{0}(\mathscr{C})\to K_{0}(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N})\to 0
of their Grothendieck groups.

Theorem
2.9
is a special case of a very recent result of Enomoto–Saito
[
ES22
, Cor. 4.32]
about extriangulated localizations.
We establish a more general version of Theorem
2.9
in Section
3
and consequently recover
[
ES22
, Cor. 4.32]
; see Corollary
3.7
and Corollary
5.14
.
Our results are framed in terms of weak Waldhausen additive categories that we introduce in Section
2.2
. Furthermore, the results are motivated by a theorem very related to Theorem
2.9
but for Waldhausen categories as we now begin to recall.

Definition 2.10
.

[
Wei13
, p. 181]

Let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
be a Waldhausen category.
An object
C\in\mathscr{C}
is called
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic
if the zero morphism
0\rightarrowtail C
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
the full subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
consisting of all
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic objects.

Suppose
\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})\to(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is an inclusion of a Waldhausen subcategory, i.e.
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
and
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
are Waldhausen categories such that
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
It is known that
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
inherits a natural Waldhausen structure from
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
, giving rise to a Waldhausen subcategory
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})
of
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
,
where

\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})^{\to}
, and

\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})^{\to}
; see
[
Wei13
, p. 181]
.
Note that if
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\twoheadrightarrow C
is a cofibration sequence in
\mathscr{C}
with
A,B\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
then it is automatic that
C\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}

(see Lemma
2.20
for details of a similar argument).
In particular, the inclusion functor
\mathsf{inc}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}
is an exact functor
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})\hookrightarrow(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
and

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}}

is a sequence of Waldhausen subcategories.

The next definition is inspired by
[
Sar20
, Thm. 6.3]
.

Definition 2.11
.

Suppose that
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
and
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
are Waldhausen categories with
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
We call (
2.1
)
a
localization sequence
in
\mathsf{Wald}

if the functor

\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}
satisfies the following property:
for each Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
satisfying the saturation and extension axioms and for each exact functor

F\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
with
F(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})\subseteq(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}
,
we have that

F
extends to an exact functor
F\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
, i.e.
F(\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

It is natural to ask when the sequence (
2.1
) becomes a localization sequence in
\mathsf{Wald}
.
The following result, which can be regarded as the
K_{0}
-part of Schlichting’s cylinder-free localization theorem
[
Sch06
, Thm. 11]
, gives us a partial answer. In this case, the functor
K_{0}\colon\mathsf{Wald}\to\mathsf{Ab}
sends such a localization sequence to a right exact sequence of Grothendieck groups.
A direct proof at the level of
K_{0}
can be found in
[
Wei13
]
and, on inspection, one can weaken the hypotheses of
[
Wei13
, Ch. II, Thm. 9.6]
to obtain the same right exact sequence of Grothendieck groups.
However, by one of our main results in Section
3
, we also see that there is an associated localization sequence of Waldhausen categories.
The proof of the following is given after Theorem
3.6
.

Theorem 2.12
.

(cf.
[
Wei13
, Ch. II, Thm. 9.6]
)
Consider the sequence (
2.1
).
Assume
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
satisfies the saturation axiom,
and that every morphism in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is the composition of a morphism from
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
followed by a morphism from
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}
.
Then (
2.1
) is a localization sequence in
\mathsf{Wald}
and induces the following right exact sequence in
\mathsf{Ab}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{inc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}})}

2.2.
Weak Waldhausen categories

Since the conflations of an extriangulated category are not kernel-cokernel pairs (but only weak-kernel-weak-cokernel pairs) in general, but cofibration sequences in Waldhausen categories are cokernel sequences,
we cannot expect to equip an extriangulated category with a Waldhausen structure in general.
We propose a more general framework in this subsection, which is fundamental in the remainder of the article.

Although we will compare the theory developed in this subsection to that of Section
2.1
(see Proposition
2.23
and Remark
2.24
),
we delay providing examples that are not Waldhausen until Section
5
in which extriangulated category theory will arm us with novel examples.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category (with a distinguished zero object
0
) equipped with a class
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\to\to}
of
distinguished sequences
of the form

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathscr{C}
, and a class
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.
Denote by
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
) the class consisting of morphisms
f
(resp.
g
) such that there is a distinguished sequence of the form (
2.2
).
The morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathsf{F}})
are called
cofibrations
(resp.
fibrations
) and denoted by

(resp.
).
The morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
are called
weak equivalences
and are denoted by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
.

(1)

The triplet
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is called a
weak Waldhausen (additive) category
if the following axioms are satisfied.

(WC0)

The class
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
is closed under composition and contains each isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}
.

(WC1)

\mathsf{Seq}
contains all split exact sequences and is closed under isomorphism.
Any distinguished sequence (
2.2
) is a weak cokernel sequence, in that
g
is a weak cokernel of
f
.
For a cofibration
f
, the corresponding fibrations are also called
distinguished weak cokernels
.

(WC2)

Any pair
(f,c)
of a cofibration
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B
and a morphism
A\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}C
yields a cofibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus C.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f\\
-c\end{pmatrix}}

Furthermore, the associated distinguished sequences of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f\\
-c\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b,\amph g\end{pmatrix}}

satisfy that
g
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
.

(WW0)

The class
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is closed under composition and contains each isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}
.

(WW1)

(Gluing Axiom) Suppose that

(2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

is a commutative diagram
in which all vertical arrows are weak equivalences and the feathered arrows are cofibrations, and that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}\oplus C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f_{i}\\
-c_{i}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b_{i},\amph g_{i}\end{pmatrix}}

are distinguished sequences for
i=1,2
.
Then there is a weak equivalence

w\colon D_{1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}D_{2}
such that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}\oplus C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
-c_{1}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b_{1},\amph g_{1}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\oplus z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f_{2}\\
-c_{2}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b_{2},\amph g_{2}\end{pmatrix}}

(2)

The triplet
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is called a
weak coWaldhausen (additive) category

if the triplet
(\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\mathsf{op}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\mathsf{op}})
is a weak Waldhausen additive category.

(3)

The triplet
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is called a
weak biWaldhausen (additive) category
if
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is both weak Waldhausen and weak coWaldhausen.

(4)

A weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is said to satisfy the
saturation axiom
if
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
satisfies Definition
2.6
(1)
.

(5)

A weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is said to satisfy the
extension axiom
if it satisfies the analogue of Definition
2.6
(2)
for distinguished sequences.

As the name suggests, the notion of a weak Waldhausen category is rather weaker than the classical notion since the fibrations are now only
weak
cokernels. Thus, an increased level of care is needed in arguments.

Remark 2.14
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a weak Waldhausen category.

(1)

As for Waldhausen categories, note that the classes
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
of cofibrations and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
of weak equivalences induce subcategories of
\mathscr{C}
; see Remark
2.4
.

(2)

Since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{C}}

is split exact, axiom
(1)(WC1)
implies
0\rightarrowtail C
is a cofibration for any
C\in\mathscr{C}
.

(3)

Suppose
f\colon A\rightarrowtail B
is a cofibration and
c\colon A\to C
is any morphism. Then, by
(1)(WC2)
, there is a distinguished sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f\\
-c\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b,\amph g\end{pmatrix}}

with
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
. Since this distinguished sequence is a weak cokernel sequence, it gives rise to a
weak pushout square
as follows.

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(wPO)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

That is, given morphisms
d\in\mathscr{C}(C,E)
and
e\in\mathscr{C}(B,E)
with
dc=ef
, there exists (a not necessarily unique)
h\in\mathscr{C}(D,E)
such that
hg=d
and
hb=e
.
We note that the object
D
is not uniquely determined, even up to isomorphism.
Therefore, a given cofibration may possibly admit non-isomorphic distinguished weak cokernels. However, these weak cokernels are all
weakly equivalent
(i.e. there is a weak equivalence between them) by
(1)(WW1)
; see also
(WW1’)
in Lemma
2.18
.

Notice that if
\begin{pmatrix}b,&g\end{pmatrix}

is a cokernel of

\begin{pmatrix}f\\
-c\end{pmatrix}

(i.e. if
\begin{pmatrix}b,&g\end{pmatrix}

is also epic),
then (
2.4
) is a pushout square, with the usual universal property including uniqueness.

Remark 2.15
.

If
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is weak coWaldhausen, then the concept dual to a weak pushout exists.
For any pair
(f,c)
of a fibration
B\overset{f}{\twoheadrightarrow}C
and a morphism
D\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}C
, there exists a distinguished sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b\\
g\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f,\amph-c\end{pmatrix}}

with
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
.
The associated commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(wPB)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

will be referred to as a
weak pullback of
f
along
c
.

Remark 2.16
.

We note that specifying a class of distinguished sequences in Definition
2.13
is essential for our purposes.
Indeed, if we were to specify only a collection of cofibrations and define weak Waldhausen structures by simply replacing pushouts in the definition of a Waldhausen category (i.e. in Definition
2.1
) with weak pushouts,
then triangulated categories would yield undesirable phenomena.
For instance, given a distinguished triangle

A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\longrightarrow A[1]
in a triangulated category
\mathscr{C}
,
the sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\oplus D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}g\\
0\end{pmatrix}}

is a weak cokernel sequence for any object
D\in\mathscr{C}
.
Then
(1)(WW1)
would force
C
to be weakly equivalent to
C\oplus D
. Since we would want
\mathscr{C}
equipped with
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}=\mathscr{C}^{\to}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{C}
to be weak Waldhausen, this would mean
C
would be isomorphic to
C\oplus D
, which is the problem.

This shows that the class of all weak cokernel sequences is too large to capture the intended structure.
The introduction of the class
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
of distinguished sequences solves this issue.

Equally, one could consider “distinguished weak pushouts” in the non-additive setting. But for simplicity, and especially from the perspective of our applications, we focus on the additive setting, leading to the more natural formulation of the axioms in Definition
2.13
.

Note that the first assertion in the following lemma uses neither
(1)(WW0)
nor
(1)(WW1)
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a weak Waldhausen category.
Then:

(1)

the finite direct sum of cofibrations is again a cofibration, and

(2)

the finite direct sum of weak equivalences is again a weak equivalence.

Proof.

(1)
:
Suppose
A_{i}\overset{f_{i}}{\rightarrowtail}B_{i}
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
for
i=1,2
.
Their direct sum

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\oplus f_{2}}

factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus B_{2}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\oplus\mathsf{id}_{A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{B_{1}}\oplus f_{2}}

We shall show
f_{1}\oplus\mathsf{id}_{A_{2}}
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
.
Since the sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus A_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
-\mathsf{id}_{A_{1}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{id}_{B_{1}}\amph f_{1}\amph 0\\
0\amph 0\amph\mathsf{id}_{A_{2}}\end{pmatrix}}

is split exact, it lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
by
(1)(WC1)
.
Moreover, we have that
f_{1}\oplus\mathsf{id}_{A_{2}}\colon A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\to B_{1}\oplus A_{2}
is a cofibration by
(1)(WC2)
.
Closure under isomorphism of
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
shows
\mathsf{id}_{B_{1}}\oplus f_{2}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
, and so
f_{1}\oplus f_{2}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
by
(1)(WC0)
.

(2)
:
Suppose
f_{i}\colon A_{i}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}B_{i}
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
for
i=1,2
.
Notice that we have pushout squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{id}_{A_{1}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\mathsf{id}_{A_{2}}\end{pmatrix}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{id}_{B_{1}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\mathsf{id}_{B_{2}}\end{pmatrix}}

(see Remark
2.14
(3)
), and by
(1)(WC1)

the distinguished split exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\oplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{A_{1}}\oplus\mathsf{id}_{A_{2}}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus B_{2}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{B_{1}}\oplus\mathsf{id}_{B_{2}}}

Thus, applying
(1)(WW1)
to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

shows that the unique morphism

f_{1}\oplus f_{2}\colon A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\to B_{1}\oplus B_{2}

must be a weak equivalence.
∎

We present a useful alternative version of the Gluing Axiom
(1)(WW1)
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Suppose
\mathscr{C}
is an additive category equipped with a class of distinguished sequences
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
and weak equivalences
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
Then
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
satisfies
(1)(WW1)
if and only if
it satisfies:

(WW1’)

For any pair of distinguished sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}

(
i=1,2
),
and weak equivalences

x\colon A_{1}\overset{\sim}{\to}A_{2}

and

y\colon B_{1}\overset{\sim}{\to}B_{2}

with

yf_{1}=f_{2}x
,
there exists a weak equivalence

z\colon C_{1}\overset{\sim}{\to}C_{2}

such that
zg_{1}=g_{2}y
.

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}

Proof.

Due to Lemma
2.17
(2)
, it is clear that
(WW1’)
implies
(1)(WW1)
.
For the converse, assume
(1)(WW1)
holds and that we have the hypotheses of
(WW1’)
.
Applying
(1)(WW1)
to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

and the distinguished sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}

(
i=1,2
)
shows that
(WW1’)
holds.
∎

We introduce some concepts for weak Waldhausen categories by analogy to the classical theory
(cf. Definitions
2.5
and
2.10
).

Definition 2.19
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
and
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
be weak Waldhausen categories.

(1)

An additive functor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is called an
exact functor
if it preserves distinguished sequences and weak equivalences, namely,

F(\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime}

and

F(\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
hold.
Moreover, if a quasi-inverse
F^{-1}
of
F
exists and is exact, then
F
is called an
exact equivalence
.

(2)

Suppose
\mathscr{C}
is an additive subcategory of
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{inc}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is the inclusion functor.
The triplet
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is called a
weak Waldhausen (additive) subcategory
of
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
if

(i)

\mathsf{inc}
is an exact functor of weak Waldhausen categories,

(ii)

\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\to\to}
,
and

(iii)

\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\to}
.

(3)

An object
C\in\mathscr{C}
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic
if
the zero map
0\rightarrowtail C
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
the full subcategory of all
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic objects in
\mathscr{C}
.

Like the case of classical Waldhausen categories, the composition of exact functors is exact.
Thus we denote by
\mathsf{wWald}
the category of skeletally small weak Waldhausen categories and exact functors.

The definition of an exact functor between weak Waldhausen categories differs from the original one (e.g.,
[
Wei13
, Ch. II, 9.1.8]
), which is attributable to specifying a class of distinguished sequences rather than cofibrations.
However, see Remark
2.24
(1)
.

The next result allows us to define a localization sequence for weak Waldhausen categories.

Lemma 2.20
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
and
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
are weak Waldhausen categories with
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
Consider the subcategory
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic objects.
There is a weak Waldhausen subcategory
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})
of
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
, and a sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}

(2.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}}

of weak Waldhausen subcategories where the first arrow is the canonical inclusion.

Proof.

First, the subcategory
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
is full by definition,
additive by Lemma
2.17
(2)
, and closed under isomorphisms by
(1)(WW0)
.

Second, we claim that if

A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}C
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
with
A,B\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
, then
C\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
.
Indeed, applying
(WW1’)
(see Lemma
2.18
) for
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

shows that the (unique) morphism
0\to C
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
and so
C
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic.

Thus, define

\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})^{\to\to}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})^{\to}.

Notice that

\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})^{\to}
,
where
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
is the class of cofibrations of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
(see Definition
2.13
).
Axioms
(1)(WC0)
,
(1)(WC1)
and
(1)(WW0)
are all clear.
Axioms
(1)(WC2)
and
(1)(WW1)
follow from those for
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
using the claim above.
Hence,
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})
is a weak Waldhausen category.
The remaining assertions are clear.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

Note that in Lemma
2.20
, if
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is weak coWaldhausen, then so too is
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})
.
This is because, in this case, if
A\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow C
is in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
with
B,C\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
, then it follows that
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
.

We make the following definition as a generalization of Definition
2.11
.

Definition 2.22
.

We call the sequence (
2.6
) a
localization sequence
in
\mathsf{wWald}
if it satisfies the analogue of Definition
2.11
for weak Waldhausen categories.

We conclude Section
2.2
by comparing the definitions introduced here with the corresponding notions for classical Waldhausen categories from Section
2.1
.
First, we characterise those weak Waldhausen categories that are Waldhausen, and show that the notion of a weak Waldhausen category indeed generalizes the classical notion.

Proposition 2.23
.

The following statements hold.

(1)

If
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a weak Waldhausen category where each distinguished sequence is a cokernel sequence, then
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a Waldhausen category.

(2)

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a Waldhausen category, and define
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
to be the collection of all cofibration sequences. Then
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a weak Waldhausen category in which all the distinguished sequences are cokernel sequences.

Proof.

(1)
:
Note that
(C0)
,
(C1)
and
(W0)
follow from
(1)(WC0)
,
(1)(WC1)
and
(1)(WW0)
, respectively.
Since each distinguished sequence is a cokernel sequence and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
is closed under isomorphism, we see that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
contains
all
cokernel sequences

A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\to C

for each cofibration
f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
.
Combining this observation with
(1)(WC2)
(resp.
(1)(WW1)
) yields
(C2)
(resp.
(W1)
).

(2)
:
First, note that
(1)(WC0)
,
(1)(WW0)
and
(1)(WW1)
follow from
(C0)
,
(W0)
and
(W1)
, respectively.

For
(1)(WC1)
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
contains all split exact sequences by Lemma
2.7
and it is clear that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
consists of weak cokernel sequences. It is closed under isomorphism by Remark
2.2
(3)
.

For
(1)(WC2)
, suppose
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B
is a cofibration (i.e. belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
) and
A\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}C
is any morphism. Then the morphism
\binom{f}{-c}
is equal to the composite

(f\oplus\mathsf{id}_{C})\circ\binom{\mathsf{id}_{A}}{-c}
.
By Lemma
2.17
(1)
, we have
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
is closed under direct sums so
f\oplus\mathsf{id}_{C}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
.
By Lemma
2.7
, the morphism
\binom{\mathsf{id}_{A}}{-c}
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\oplus C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{id}_{A}\\
-c\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}c,\amph\mathsf{id}_{C}\end{pmatrix}}

is split exact.
Hence, the composite is also a cofibration by
(C0)
.
Then
(C2)
, and that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
are closed under isomorphisms, imply there is a cofibration (i.e. distinguished) sequence of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbin{\amalg}_{A}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f\\
-c\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b,\amph g\end{pmatrix}}

with
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
.
∎

In the setup of Proposition
2.23
(2)
, we say that
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is the
corresponding weak Waldhausen category
of the Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.

Remark 2.24
.

Suppose

(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
,

(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})

and

(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})

are Waldhausen additive categories,
with corresponding weak Waldhausen categories

(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
,

(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})

and

(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
,
respectively.
The following observations are straightforward to verify.

(1)

An additive functor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is an exact functor

F\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
of weak Waldhausen categories (as defined in Definition
2.19
)
if and only if it is an exact functor

F\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})

of Waldhausen categories (in the sense of Definition
2.5
).

(2)

Suppose
\mathscr{C}
is an additive subcategory of
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{inc}\colon\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is the inclusion functor.
Then

\mathsf{inc}\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\hookrightarrow(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
is an inclusion of a Waldhausen subcategory if and only if

\mathsf{inc}\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\hookrightarrow(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
is an inclusion of a weak Waldhausen subcategory.

(3)

Consider the Waldhausen subcategory
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})\subseteq(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})

of
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic objects.
The corresponding weak Waldhausen category of
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})
is the weak Waldhausen subcategory
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})
of
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
as produced in Lemma
2.20
.

(4)

Lastly, we note that if (
2.6
) a localization sequence of weak Waldhausen categories, then (
2.1
) is a localization sequence of Waldhausen categories (since we are assuming the categories in this remark are Waldhausen). However, the converse does not necessarily follow.

2.3.
The Grothendieck group of a weak Waldhausen category

The
Grothendieck group

K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
of a skeletally small weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is given by a straightforward adjustment of Definition
2.8
, using distinguished sequences in place of cofibration sequences.
It is also clear that an exact functor

F\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})

of weak Waldhausen categories
induces a group homomorphism

K_{0}(F)\colon K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
, which extends to a functor

K_{0}\colon\mathsf{wWald}\to\mathsf{Ab}
.

We end this subsection with some simple examples.

Example 2.25
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a skeletally small Waldhausen category with corresponding weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
. Then it is clear that

K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})=K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.

Example 2.26
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a skeletally small abelian category.
Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
denote the class of all right exact sequences

A\overset{f}{\to}B\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}C
,
namely,
sequences where
g
is a cokernel of
f
;
and put
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{C}
, the class of all isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.
Then
(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a weak Waldhausen additive category.
Indeed, since
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}=\mathscr{A}^{\to}
, the conditions
(1)(WC0)
and
(1)(WW0)
are clear.
The other conditions also follow from this and the fact that all distinguished sequences are cokernel sequences. Furthermore, Proposition
2.23
(1)
tells us that
(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a Waldhausen category.

Lastly, in this example, we have
K_{0}(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\cong 0
. Indeed, as any object
C\in\mathscr{A}
fits into a distinguished sequence
C\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow 0
, we have
[C]=0
in
K_{0}(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.

3.
Localization theorems for weak Waldhausen categories

Having established the framework of weak Waldhausen categories, we can state and prove our two main localization results, namely, Theorem
3.6
and Corollary
3.7
, in this section.
These show that, under certain assumptions on
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
, the sequence (
3.1
) below is a localization sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}
, which in turn induces a right exact sequence of their Grothendieck groups.
Our localization results are based on Corollary
2.12
.
To accurately handle the Serre and Verdier quotients in terms of weak Waldhausen categories, we introduce the
homotopy category
of a weak Waldhausen category in Section
3.1
.
Under some conditions, we show that the Grothendieck group of a weak Waldhausen category is isomorphic to that of its homotopy category (when it exists).
This will be used to prove Corollary
5.14
.

We work under the following setup throughout Section
3
.

Setup 3.1
.

We assume there is a sequence

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}}

of skeletally small weak Waldhausen subcategories, where
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is the subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
-acyclic objects in
\mathscr{C}
.
We put
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}}
.

To compare the Grothendieck groups of
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
and
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
, we introduce certain classes of morphisms (cf. Definition
4.13
).

Definition 3.2
.

We define the following four classes of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
.

(i)

\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}

(ii)

\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}_{\mathsf{sec}}\coloneqq\Set{f\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}}{f\text{ is a section}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}

(iii)

\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{F}}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}

(iv)

\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\set{g\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}}{\text{there is a sequence $A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}C$ in $\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}$ with $A\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}$}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}

For the proof of Theorem
3.6
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}
and

A\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}f\\
\sim\end{subarray}}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}C
is a distinguished sequence in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
,
then the morphisms
0\to C
and
C\to 0
both lie in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
. In particular,
C\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
.

Proof.

Apply
(WW1’)
(see Lemma
2.18
) to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

∎

Remark 3.4
.

For an arbitrary weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
, the converse of the first assertion of Lemma
3.3
may fail.
For instance, let
(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
denote the weak Waldhausen category, where

\mathscr{A}
is an abelian category with at least one proper epimorphism
f\colon A\to B
(i.e.
f
is epic but not monic),

\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
is the collection of all right exact sequences in
\mathscr{A}
, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{C}
.
Then
f
is a cofibration in
(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
; see Example
2.26
.
However, even though the distinguished weak cokernel of
f
is (up to isomorphism)
B\to 0
and
0\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
, the morphism
f
does not lie in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{A}
and hence
f\notin\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is weak biWaldhausen.
If
g\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}
and

A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}g\\
\sim\end{subarray}}{\twoheadrightarrow}C
is in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
,
then
0\to A
and
A\to 0
both belong to
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}=\mathcal{T}
in this case.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
3.3
to the distinguished sequence

C\overset{g^{\mathsf{op}}}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{f^{\mathsf{op}}}{\twoheadrightarrow}A

in the weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\mathsf{op}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\mathsf{op}})
, where
g^{\mathsf{op}}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\mathsf{op}}
.
∎

We can now prove our main results of Section
3
.
These show that the functor
K_{0}\colon\mathsf{wWald}\to\mathsf{Ab}
sends certain localization sequences to right exact sequences. Recall we are assuming Setup
3.1
.

Theorem 3.6

(Weak Waldhausen Localization Theorem)
.

Assume that
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
consists of finite compositions of morphisms from
\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}\cup\mathcal{T}\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}
.
Then (
3.1
) is a localization sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}
that induces a right exact sequence in
\mathsf{Ab}
as follows.

(3.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\coloneqq K_{0}(\mathsf{inc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\coloneqq K_{0}(\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}})}

Proof.

First, we confirm that (
3.1
) is a localization sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}
.
Thus, suppose
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
is a weak Waldhausen category satisfying the saturation and extension axioms, and also that
H\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
is an exact functor with
H(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})\subseteq(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}
.
Since
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
and
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
have the same underlying category and the same distinguished sequences, we need only show
H(\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
.
Since
H
is exact, we know
H(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
.

To show
H(\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
,
consider
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}
together with a distinguished sequence
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\twoheadrightarrow C
, where
C\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
by Lemma
3.3
.
There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hf}

of distinguished sequences in
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
.
By the assumption, we know
HC\in(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}
which shows
0\to HC
is in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
.
Thus, by the extension axiom, the morphism
HA\overset{Hf}{\rightarrowtail}HB
also lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
.

Lastly, consider a morphism
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
which admits
a distinguished sequence

A^{\prime}\overset{f^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}B^{\prime}\overset{g^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}C^{\prime}

with
A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
. There is a commutative diagram

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HB^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HB^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HA^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HB^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HC^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hg^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hf^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hg^{\prime}}

in
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
of sequences in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime}
, where
HA^{\prime}\in(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}}
.
Then
(WW1’)
(see Lemma
2.18
) implies
Hg^{\prime}
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
, noting that an induced morphism
HB^{\prime}\to HC^{\prime}
must be equal to
Hg^{\prime}
in order to make (
3.3
) commute.
We have thus verified
H(\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
, and hence (
3.1
) is indeed a localization sequence.

Next we verify the last assertion.
Since (
3.1
) sits in
\mathsf{wWald}
, we have the induced group homomorphisms
F
and
G
in (
3.2
).
Since
0\to A
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
for any object
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
, the composition
F\circ G
vanishes.
Thus,
G
factors uniquely through the cokernel
\operatorname{Cok}F
of
F
as follows.

(3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cok}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{isoclass}}(\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\exists!}G^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

The homomorphisms
\phi
and
\psi
are the canonical surjective maps,
and
\psi
factors uniquely as
G\phi
because
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
We shall show that there exists an inverse of
G^{\prime}
by showing that
P\phi
must factor uniquely through
\psi
.

First note that for any distinguished sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
, we have

P[A]-P[B]+P[C]=0
in
\operatorname{Cok}F
, because already

\phi[A]-\phi[B]+\phi[C]=0
.

Second, let
f\colon A\to B
be a morphism in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
. To see that

P[A]=P[B]
, it suffices to consider the three cases since
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
consists of compositions from
\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}\cup\mathcal{T}\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}
.

(1)

If
f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}
, then we see that
[A]=[B]
already in
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
, and hence
P[A]=P[B]
in
\operatorname{Cok}F
.

(2)

If
f\in\mathcal{T}
, then there is a distinguished sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}

with
K\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
.
In particular,
P[K]=0
in
\operatorname{Cok}F
and hence the equality

[A]=[K]+[B]
in
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})

implies

P[A]=P[B]
in
\operatorname{Cok}F
.

(3)

If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}
, then we have a distinguished sequence
A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\twoheadrightarrow C
with
C\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
by Lemma
3.3
. As above we deduce
P[A]=P[B]
in
\operatorname{Cok}F
.

Hence, there exists a unique homomorphism
G^{\prime\prime}\colon K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to\operatorname{Cok}F
with
P=G^{\prime\prime}\circ G
by the universal property of
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
using that
\phi
is epic.
This proves that
G^{\prime}
is an isomorphism and that (
3.2
) is exact.
∎
