Now we can see that Corollary
2.12
, a localization theorem for classical Waldhausen categories, is a consequence of Theorem
3.6
.

Proof of Corollary
2.12
.

Due to Proposition
2.23
(2)
, we know that the Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
corresponds to a weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
. Since
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{C}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
satisfies the saturation axiom, if
w=vf
where
w\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
,
v\in\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
and
f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}
, we see that
f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}
. The result then follows from an application of Theorem
3.6
, noting Remark
2.24
(3)
and Example
2.25
.
∎

In the case of biWaldhausen structures, we can slightly relax the assumption on
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.

Corollary 3.7

(Weak biWaldhausen Localization Theorem)
.

Assume that (
3.1
) is a sequence of weak biWaldhausen categories.
If
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
consists of finite compositions of morphisms from
\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}
,
then (
3.1
) is a localization sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}
and (
3.2
) is exact.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.5
, this follows immediately from Theorem
3.6
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

Note that in Theorem
3.6
, and hence also in Corollary
3.7
, we did not need the saturation axiom for
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
.

3.1.
The homotopy category

This subsection contains a preparatory result relating the Grothendieck group of a weak Waldhausen category to that of its homotopy category (when it exists). This will be used to prove Corollary
5.14
.

Recall that Setup
3.1
is in play.
Let
\overline{(-)}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}/[\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}]
denote
the additive quotient functor and
define
\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}\coloneqq\set{\overline{w}\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}^{\to}}{w\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
.
Consider the localization functors
Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]

and

\overline{Q}\colon\overline{\mathscr{C}}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
.
Note that we cannot deduce these localizations are additive without further assumptions (see Setup
3.10
). However, we do have the following.

Lemma 3.9
.

There is an isomorphism
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]\cong\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
of categories.

Proof.

We consider the
saturation
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
, namely,

\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}\coloneqq\set{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\to}}{Q(f)\text{\ is an isomorphism in\ }\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]}\supseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}},

and establish the associated localization
Q_{0}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}^{-1}]
.
It is clear that the class
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}
is
saturated
, that is,
for each
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\to}
we have

Q(f)
is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}^{-1}]
if and only if
f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}
.
Then, by the universality of
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]
and
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}^{-1}]
, we have an isomorphism
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]\cong\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}^{-1}]
.

The ideal quotient
\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is the localization
\mathscr{C}[\mathsf{S}^{-1}]
with respect to the class
\mathsf{S}
of retractions in
\mathscr{C}^{\to}
that admit a kernel in
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
; see
[
Oga22a
, the dual of Exam. 2.6]
.
We claim that
\mathsf{S}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}
.
Indeed, by e.g
[
Sha20
, Prop. 2.7]
,
any morphism in
\mathsf{S}
is isomorphic to one of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\oplus A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}0,\amph\mathsf{id}_{A}\end{pmatrix}}

where
N\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
. In particular,
0\colon 0\to N
is in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
, and hence

0\oplus\mathsf{id}_{A}\colon 0\oplus A\to N\oplus A

also lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}
by Lemma
2.17
(2)
.
The composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\oplus A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\oplus A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\oplus\mathsf{id}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}0,\amph\mathsf{id}_{A}\end{pmatrix}}

is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}
, so
we have

\begin{pmatrix}0,&\mathsf{id}_{A}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}

as
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}
is saturated.

Since
Q
inverts all morphisms in
\mathsf{S}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}
,
there exists a unique functor

Q_{1}\colon\overline{\mathscr{C}}\cong\mathscr{C}[\mathsf{S}^{-1}]\to\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}^{-1}]

satisfying
Q=Q_{1}\circ\overline{(-)}
.
Also, by the universality of
\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
, there is a unique functor
Q_{2}\colon\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]\to\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}^{-1}]
with
Q_{1}=Q_{2}\overline{Q}
.
Lastly, the universality of
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]
guarantees a unique functor
Q_{3}\colon\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
with
Q_{3}Q=\overline{Q}\circ\overline{(-)}
.
The diagram below summarizes the situation.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]\cong\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{0}^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}[\mathsf{S}^{-1}]\cong\overline{\mathscr{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\cong Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(-)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\exists!}Q_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\exists!}Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\exists!}Q_{2}}

One can then check that
Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
are mutually inverse functors, yielding the isomorphism
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]\cong\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
as desired.
∎

Given Lemma
3.9
, we typically identify the categories
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]
and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
. Note also that nothing is lost by assuming
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is saturated; see the proof of Lemma
3.9
.
Thus, in addition to Setup
3.1
, we assume the following for the rest of Section
3
.

Setup 3.10
.

Suppose
\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
is a multiplicative system in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
and the class
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is saturated.

Under Setup
3.10
, it follows from
[
GZ67
, I.3.3]
that
\overline{Q}\colon\overline{\mathscr{C}}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
is an additive functor of additive categories.
Thus, in turn, the localization
Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]
is also additive, which allows us to consider weak Waldhausen structures on
\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}]
.

Definition 3.11
.

We call a triplet

(\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}],\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
the
weak Waldhausen (additive) homotopy category of
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
if:

(i)

(\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}],\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
is a weak Waldhausen category;

(ii)

\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime}
coincides with the isomorphism closure of
Q(\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}})
; and

(iii)

\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}(\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}])
.

In this case, we use the notation
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
to denote the triplet
(\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}],\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
.
Note also that
Q\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is an exact functor in
\mathsf{wWald}
.

We may immediately prove the following.

Proposition 3.12
.

If
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
exists, then

K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\cong K_{0}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}))
.

Proof.

Since the localization functor

Q\colon(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})

is exact,
it induces a group homomorphism

K_{0}(Q)\colon K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to K_{0}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}))
.

We shall construct an inverse of
K_{0}(Q)
.
Since

\operatorname{Ob}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})=\operatorname{Ob}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}))
,
there is a canonical map

\operatorname{\mathsf{isoclass}}(\mathscr{C})\to K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})

given by
[X]\mapsto[X]
,
which we claim respects the generating relations of

K_{0}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}))
.

First, suppose
\alpha\colon A\to B
is in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}(\mathscr{C}[\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{-1}])
, hence also an isomorphism in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}^{-1}]
.
As
\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
is a multiplicative system (see Setup
3.10
),
there is a roof diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{u}}

in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
representing
\alpha
with

u\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
Since
\alpha
is an isomorphism and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is saturated (see Setup
3.10
),
we actually have
t\in\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
also,
so
[A]=[B^{\prime}]=[B]
in

K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.

Second, suppose

A\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow C

is a distinguished sequence
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
, which by definition is thus
isomorphic to the image of a
distinguished sequence

A^{\prime}\rightarrowtail B^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C^{\prime}

in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
.
For
X=A,B,C
, as argued above for
\alpha
,
there is a roof diagram from
X
to
X^{\prime}

consisting of morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
.
In particular, we have

[A]-[B]+[C]=[A^{\prime}]-[B^{\prime}]+[C^{\prime}]=0
.
Hence, there is an induced group homomorphism

\psi\colon K_{0}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}))\to K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
that is clearly inverse to
K_{0}(Q)
.
∎

4.
Extriangulated categories

We recall some details on extriangulated categories in this section; see
[
NP19
]
for more details.
An
extriangulated category
is a triplet
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
consisting of:

(1)

an additive category
\mathscr{C}
,

(2)

a biadditive functor
\mathbb{E}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathsf{Ab}
, and

(3)

an assignment
\mathfrak{s}
that,
for all
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
and each element
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
,
associates an equivalence class
\mathfrak{s}(\delta)=[A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C]
of a pair
(f,g)\in\mathscr{C}^{\to\to}
.

(The equivalence mentioned in
(3)
above is the usual Yoneda equivalence for
3
-term sequences; see
[
NP19
, Def. 2.7]
.)
Moreover, this triplet must satisfy the axioms
(ET
1
)–
(ET4)
, (ET
3)^{\mathsf{op}}
and (ET
4)^{\mathsf{op}}
as
detailed in
[
NP19
, Def. 2.12]
.
We recall the
(ET4)
axiom after Definition
4.1
since it used several times later.

For the remainder of Section
4
, let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category.

Definition 4.1
.

The following terminology is now standard in extriangulated category theory; it is motivated by that used for exact categories (see
[
Kel90
, App. A]
) and for triangulated categories.

(1)

Let
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
be objects and suppose
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.
Then
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
is known as an
\mathbb{E}
-extension
.
For morphisms
a\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and
c\colon C^{\prime}\to C
in
\mathscr{C}
, we obtain two new
\mathbb{E}
-extensions

a_{*}\delta\coloneqq\mathbb{E}(C,a)(\delta)\in\mathbb{E}(C,A^{\prime})
and
c^{*}\delta\coloneqq\mathbb{E}(c,A)(\delta)\in\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime},A)
.

(2)

Suppose
\mathfrak{s}(\delta)=[A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C]
for some
\mathbb{E}
-extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.
Then
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C
is called an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation
, and
the morphisms
f
and
g
are called an
\mathfrak{s}
-inflation
and an
\mathfrak{s}
-deflation
, respectively.
This situation is usually summarised by the diagram

A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
, which is known as an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
.

(3)

A
morphism
of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles
from

A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}

to

A^{\prime}\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}B^{\prime}\overset{g^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\overset{\delta^{\prime}}{\dashrightarrow}

is a triplet
(a,b,c)
of morphisms

a\in\mathscr{C}(A,A^{\prime})
,

b\in\mathscr{C}(B,B^{\prime})
and

c\in\mathscr{C}(C,C^{\prime})

such that
a_{*}\delta=c^{*}\delta^{\prime}
and so that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}}

In contrast to an exact category, an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation is not a kernel-cokernel pair in general, but only a weak-kernel-weak-cokernel pair (see
[
NP19
, Prop. 3.3]
).
As expected,
\mathfrak{s}
-conflations play the role of short exact sequences or of distinguished triangles; see Examples
4.3
and
4.4
.

With this terminology in place, let us recall
(ET4)
which holds for
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
; (ET
4)^{\mathsf{op}}
is dual.

(ET4)

Let
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}D\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
and
B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{g^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}F\overset{\rho}{\dashrightarrow}
be
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.
Then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\coloneqq g\circ f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{*}\rho}

in
\mathscr{C}
, where

A\overset{h}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{h^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}E\overset{\delta^{\prime}}{\dashrightarrow}
and
D\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}E\overset{e}{\longrightarrow}F\overset{f^{\prime}_{*}\rho}{\dashrightarrow}
are
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles,
and

d^{*}\delta^{\prime}=\delta
and
e^{*}\rho=f_{*}\delta^{\prime}
.

An extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is simply denoted by
\mathscr{C}
if there is no confusion.

The next lemma will be used in many places, and it follows from
[
LN19
, Prop. 1.20]
(see also
[
NP19
, Cor. 3.16]
,
[
HS20
, Prop. 2.22]
).
It says that, like in the triangulated case, extriangulated categories admit weak pushouts and weak pullbacks.

Lemma 4.2
.

The following properties hold.

(1)

For any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
together with a morphism
a\in\mathscr{C}(A,A^{\prime})
and an associated
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
A^{\prime}\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}B^{\prime}\overset{g^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{a_{*}\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
,
there exists
b\in\mathscr{C}(B,B^{\prime})
which gives a morphism of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(wPO)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\delta}

and makes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f\\
a\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b,\amph-f^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g^{\prime})^{*}\delta}

an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle.
Furthermore, the commutative square
{\rm(wPO)}
is a weak pushout of
f
along
a
(in the sense of Remark
2.14
(3)
).

(2)

Dually, for any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
together with a morphism
c\in\mathscr{C}(C^{\prime},C)
and an associated
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
A\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}B^{\prime}\overset{g^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}C^{\prime}\overset{c^{*}\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
,
there exists
b\in\mathscr{C}(B^{\prime},B)
which gives a morphism of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{(wPB)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}\delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

and makes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}\oplus B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}-g^{\prime}\\
b\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}c,\amph g\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{*}\delta}

an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle.
The commutative square
{\rm(wPB)}
is a weak pullback of
g
along
c

(in the sense of Remark
2.15
).

We recall how exact categories and triangulated categories naturally give rise to extriangulated categories.

Example 4.3
.

[
NP19
, Exam. 2.13]

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E})
be an exact category, such that
the collection

\mathbb{E}(C,A)\coloneqq\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}(C,A)=\Set{[A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C]}{A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\text{ lies in }\mathcal{E}}

is a set for all
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
.
(This set-theoretic requirement is met for example if
\mathscr{C}
is skeletally small, or if
\mathscr{C}
has enough projectives or enough injectives.)
We can extend this definition of
\mathbb{E}
on objects in
\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathscr{C}
to a biadditive functor
\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathsf{Ab}
using pushouts and pullbacks of (admissible) conflations.
Declare that
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{}{\dashrightarrow}

is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
if and only if

A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C

is a kernel-cokernel pair in
\mathcal{E}
.
Then
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an extriangulated category and we say it
corresponds to
or
is an exact category
.
As a special case, if
\mathscr{C}
is an abelian category and
\mathcal{E}
consists of all the short exact sequences in
\mathscr{C}
, then we say
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})

corresponds to
or
is an abelian category
.

Example 4.4
.

[
NP19
, Prop. 3.22]

Let
(\mathscr{C},[1],\triangle)
be a triangulated category, where
[1]
is the suspension automorphism and
\triangle
is the triangulation consisting of (distinguished) triangles.
Define
\mathbb{E}(C,A)\coloneqq\mathscr{C}(C,A[1])
for
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
. Thus, the
\mathbb{E}
-extensions in this case are just morphisms.
If
a\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and
c\colon C^{\prime}\to C
are morphisms, and
h\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
, then

a_{*}h=\mathbb{E}(C,a)(h)=a[1]\circ h

and

c^{*}h=\mathbb{E}(c,A)(h)=hc
.
This yields a biadditive functor
\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathsf{Ab}
.
Lastly, declare that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
if and only if

A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{h}{\longrightarrow}A[1]

is a triangle in
\mathscr{C}
.
Then
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an extriangulated category and we say it
corresponds to
or
is a triangulated category
.

4.1.
The category
\mathsf{Extri}
of extriangulated categories

Just as for exact and triangulated categories, there is a notion of an exact functor between extriangulated categories.

Definition 4.5
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
,
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
and
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime})
are extriangulated categories.

(1)

[
BTS21
, Def. 2.32]

An
exact
(or
extriangulated
)
functor
from

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
to
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})

is a pair
(F,\phi)
of an additive functor
F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
and a natural transformation
\phi\colon\mathbb{E}\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\circ(F^{\mathsf{op}}\times F)
,
such that if

A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}

is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle, then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fg}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C,A}(\delta)}

is an
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
-triangle.

(2)

[
NOS22
, Def. 2.11(2)]

Suppose
(F,\phi)\colon(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
and
(F^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\colon(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime})
are exact functors.
Then the composition

(F^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\circ(F,\phi)\colon(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime})

is the exact functor
(F^{\prime\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime})
given by

F^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq F^{\prime}\circ F

and

\phi^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq(\phi^{\prime}\cdot(F^{\mathsf{op}}\times F))\circ\phi
,
where
\phi^{\prime}\cdot(F^{\mathsf{op}}\times F)
denotes the whiskering of
\phi^{\prime}
with
F^{\mathsf{op}}\times F
.

If
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
and
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
are exact (resp. triangulated) categories, then
an exact functor
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})

coincides with the classical notion of an exact functor between exact categories (resp. an exact or triangulated functor between triangulated categories); see
[
BTS21
, Thms. 2.34 and 2.33]
.

Exact functors between extriangulated categories allow us to consider the category of extriangulated categories.

Definition 4.6
.

We denote by
\mathsf{Extri}
the category of skeletally small extriangulated categories and exact functors between them.
In addition, we let
\mathsf{Tri}
,
\mathsf{Exa}
and
\mathsf{Abel}
, respectively, denote the full subcategories of
\mathsf{Extri}
consisting of triangulated, exact and abelian categories, respectively.

We restrict to skeletally small categories because we will usually be considering localizations or Grothendieck groups.
If one further restricts to small extriangulated categories, then we obtain the
2
-category of small extriangulated categories, exact functors, and morphisms of extriangulated functors as introduced in
[
NOS22
, Def. 2.11(3)]
.
This
2
-category-theoretic viewpoint has been developed in
[
BTHSS23
]
for
n
-exangulated categories, which are a generalisation of extriangulated categories to higher homological algebra.

It is well known that an extension-closed subcategory of an exact category inherits an exact structure from the ambient category, whereas the analogue is not necessarily true for an extension-closed subcategory of a triangulated category. That is,
\mathsf{Exa}
is closed under taking extension-closed subcategories but
\mathsf{Tri}
is not.
A similar issue arises when taking relative theories.
Moreover, both
\mathsf{Exa}
and
\mathsf{Tri}
are not closed under general localizations in a useful sense.
Extriangulated category theory has the flexibility to be closed under all these operations.

In the next two subsections, we recall the needed details about extension-closure and the localization theory of extriangulated categories.
We recall the theory about relative structures later in Section
6
where it will be used extensively.

4.2.
Extension-closed subcategories

Let
\mathscr{N}
be a subcategory of the extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
. Then
\mathscr{N}
is said to be
extension-closed
if it satisfies the following:

(i)

\mathscr{N}
is full, additive and closed under isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}
, and

(ii)

if
A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation with
A,C\in\mathscr{N}
, then
B\in\mathscr{N}
.

The following is then easily verified.

Proposition 4.7
.

[
NP19
, Rem. 2.18]

Let
\mathscr{N}
be an extension-closed subcategory of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
. Then there is an extriangulated category
(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})
, where

\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}}
is the restriction of
\mathbb{E}
to
\mathscr{N}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathscr{N}
, and
\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}}
is the restriction of
\mathfrak{s}
to
\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}}
.
Furthermore, the inclusion functor
\mathsf{inc}\colon\mathscr{N}\to\mathscr{C}
is part of an exact functor

(\mathsf{inc},\iota)\colon(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})\to(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
, where

\iota\colon\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}}\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}

is the canonical inclusion.

4.3.
Localization theory

The localization theory of extriangulated categories was initiated in
[
NOS22
]
in order to unify Verdier
[
Ver96
]
and Serre quotients
[
Gab62
]
(see Example
4.16
). In this subsection we remain general, whereas in Section
6
we will specialize to the localization of triangulated categories.

We begin by recalling the definition of a thick subcategory of an extriangulated category and then introducing exact sequences in
\mathsf{Extri}
.

Definition 4.8
.

[
NOS22
, Def. 4.1]
Let
\mathscr{N}
be a subcategory of the extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.
Then
\mathscr{N}
is called
thick
if:

(i)

\mathscr{N}
is full, additive and closed under direct summands in
\mathscr{C}
, and

(ii)

\mathscr{N}
satisfies the
2
-out-of-
3
property
for
\mathfrak{s}
-conflations, i.e if any two of
A,B
or
C
belong to
\mathscr{N}
in an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation
A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C
, then so does the third.

It follows that a thick subcategory
\mathscr{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is closed under isomorphisms and is extension-closed. Hence,
\mathscr{N}
inherits an extriangulated structure by Proposition
4.7
and, in this case, we will sometimes write
(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})\subseteq(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is a thick subcategory.
Furthermore, if
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is a triangulated category,
then a subcategory is thick in the sense of Definition
4.8
if and only if it is thick in usual sense for triangulated categories (i.e. a triangulated subcategory that is closed under direct summands); see
[
NOS22
, Exam. 4.8]
.

Definition 4.9
.

[
Oga22b
, Def. 1.17]

A thick subcategory
\mathscr{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is called a
Serre
subcategory if any
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation
A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C
with
B\in\mathscr{N}
implies
A,C\in\mathscr{N}
.

If
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an exact category, then the notion of a Serre subcategory as in Definition
4.9
is the same as the classical definition for exact categories given in
[
CE98
, Def. 4.0.35]
.

We now introduce exact sequences of extriangulated categories in
\mathsf{Extri}
, motivated by the notion of exact sequences in
\mathsf{Tri}
and
\mathsf{Abel}
; see e.g.
[
PV18
, Sec. 2.3]
. The justification of our terminology is given by Proposition
4.17
after we have recalled extriangulated localizations.

Definition 4.10
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})\subseteq(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is thick.
We say that the sequence

(4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{inc},\iota)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\mu)}

in
\mathsf{Extri}
is an
exact sequence
of extriangulated categories,
if the following conditions are fulfilled.

(1)

\mathscr{N}=\operatorname{Im}(\mathsf{inc})=\operatorname{Ker}Q
holds.

(2)

For any exact functor

(G,\psi)\colon(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})

in
\mathsf{Extri}
with
G|_{\mathscr{N}}=G\circ\mathsf{inc}=0
,
there is a unique exact functor

(G^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{t})\to(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})

such that
(G,\psi)=(G^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\circ(Q,\mu)
.

Exact sequences in
\mathsf{Tri}
,
\mathsf{Exa}
and
\mathsf{Abel}
are defined similarly.

We will work under the following setup for the rest of Section
4
.

Setup 4.11
.

We suppose that
(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})
is a thick subcategory of a skeletally small extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

In the remainder of Subsection
4.3
, we review some sufficient conditions on the pair
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{N})
to guarantee the existence of an exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
with end-term a certain localization of
\mathscr{C}
dependent on
\mathscr{N}
.

Definition 4.12
.

Suppose
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation.
The object
A
is known as a
cocone
of the
\mathfrak{s}
-deflation
g
and
C
a
cone
of the
\mathfrak{s}
-inflation
f
.
By
[
NP19
, Rem. 3.10]
, these objects are uniquely determined up to isomorphism by
g
and
f
, respectively.
Therefore, we write
\operatorname{\mathsf{CoCone}}(g)\coloneqq A
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(f)\coloneqq C
.

Associated to
\mathscr{N}
we define several classes of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
;
we will compare Definitions
4.13
and
3.2
in Section
5
once we have seen that extriangulated categories are examples of weak Waldhausen categories (see Lemma
5.9
).

Definition 4.13
.

[
NOS22
, Def. 4.3]
,
[
Oga22b
, Lem. 2.12]

Define subsets of
\mathscr{C}^{\to}
as follows.

(i)

\mathcal{L}\coloneqq\Set{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\to}}{f\ \textnormal{is an $\mathfrak{s}$-inflation with}\ \operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(f)\in\mathscr{N}}

(ii)

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{sec}}\coloneqq\Set{f\in\mathcal{L}}{f\textnormal{ is a section}}

(iii)

\mathcal{R}\coloneqq\Set{g\in\mathscr{C}^{\to}}{g\ \textnormal{is an $\mathfrak{s}$-deflation with}\ \operatorname{\mathsf{CoCone}}(g)\in\mathscr{N}}

(iv)

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{ret}}\coloneqq\Set{g\in\mathcal{R}}{g\textnormal{ is a retraction}}

(v)

\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is the smallest subclass of
\mathscr{C}^{\to}
closed under composition containing both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}

A morphism
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\to}
lies in
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
if and only if
f
is a finite composition of morphisms from
\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{R}
; see
[
NOS22
, p. 374]
.
It is clear that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
contains all isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}
(as each isomorphism is e.g. an
\mathfrak{s}
-inflation with cone
0
), and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is closed under composition by definition. Furthermore, one can mimic the argument in Lemma
2.17
(1)
to see that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is closed under finite direct sums.
Thus,
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
satisfies:

(M0)

\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
contains
\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{C}
, and is closed under composition and taking finite direct sums.

Consider the quotient functor

\overline{(-)}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}/[\mathscr{N}]
, and define

\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}\coloneqq\Set{\overline{f}}{f\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{C}}^{\to}
.
We denote by

\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}[\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}^{-1}]\cong\overline{\mathscr{C}}[\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}^{-1}]

the localization of
\mathscr{C}
at
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
, where the isomorphism follows from the same argument as in Lemma
3.9
.
In addition, we denote the localization functor by
Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
.
Under certain conditions, it indeed gives rise to an exact functor between extriangulated categories.

Theorem 4.14
.

If
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is saturated and
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}
satisfies conditions

(MR1)
–
(MR4)
of
[
NOS22
, p. 343]
,
then there is an extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
together with an
exact functor
(Q,\mu)\colon(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})\to(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
, such that the following sequence is exact in
\mathsf{Extri}
.

(4.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{inc},\iota)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\mu)}

Proof.

This follows from
[
OS23
, Thm. 3.8]
and
[
ES22
, Prop. 4.3]
.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

We make some remarks on Theorem
4.14
.

(1)

We call the exact functor

(Q,\mu)\colon(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})\to(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})

the
extriangulated localization of
\mathscr{C}
with respect to
\mathscr{N}
.
It is denoted more simply by
Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
if the extriangulated structures are understood.
We sometimes refer to (
4.2
) as an
extriangulated localization sequence
.

(2)

Although
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
being saturated is not necessary to prove
[
NOS22
, Thm. 3.5]
, it is essential to prove our main results and hence we assume this condition from the outset. Consequently, assuming (MR1) is redundant since the saturation of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
implies (MR1).

(3)

The definition of
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}
we have used is not the original one given in
[
NOS22
, Def. 3.1]
. Indeed, let
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}^{*}
denote the closure of
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}
with respect to composition with isomorphisms. The condition
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}=\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}^{*}
is required in
[
NOS22
, Thm. 3.5]
, but this is guaranteed in our setup by
[
NOS22
, Lem. 3.2(3)]
when (MR1) is satisfied.

(4)

The condition (MR2) says that
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}
is a multiplicative system in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.

(5)

The biadditive functor
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}
and realization
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
are defined in
[
NOS22
, Secs. 3.2–3.3]
.
A description of the
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
-inflations and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
-deflations is given in
[
NOS22
, Lem. 3.32]
.

As mentioned earlier, Verdier and Serre quotients are motivating examples of extriangulated localizations; see
[
NOS22
, Sec. 4]
.

Example 4.16
.

[
NOS22
, Exams. 4.8, 4.9]

(1)

(Verdier quotient.)
Suppose
\mathscr{N}
is thick in a (skeletally small) triangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.
Then we have
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}=\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{R}
,

Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
is the localization functor for the Verdier quotient of
\mathscr{C}
by
\mathscr{N}
,
and

\mathscr{N}\to\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}

is an exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
.

(2)

(Serre quotient.)
Suppose
\mathscr{N}
is a Serre subcategory of a (skeletally small) abelian category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.
Then we have
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}=\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{R}
,

Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
is the localization functor for the Serre quotient of
\mathscr{C}
by
\mathscr{N}
,
and

\mathscr{N}\to\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}

is an exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
.

In light of Example
4.16
, we are in position to justify the choice of terminology in Definition
4.10
.

Proposition 4.17
.

If (
4.1
) is an exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
that also lies in
\mathsf{Tri}
(resp.
\mathsf{Abel}
), then it is the usual Verdier (resp. Serre) quotient.
Conversely, a Verdier (resp. Serre) quotient
\mathscr{N}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
gives rise to an exact sequence (
4.1
) in
\mathsf{Extri}
.

Proof.

First, assume (
4.1
) is an exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
in the sense of Definition
4.10
, and that

(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})
,

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})

and

(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{t})

are triangulated categories.
By definition, the subcategory
\mathscr{N}=\operatorname{Ker}Q
is thick.
Thus, we can construct the Verdier quotient
\mathscr{N}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\overset{Q^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
.
By universality of the Verdier quotient and
(2)
above, we have a triangle isomorphism
F\colon\mathscr{D}\cong\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
with
Q^{\prime}=FQ
, and thus
Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
is a Verdier quotient.

Second, assume (
4.1
) is an exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
that lies in
\mathsf{Abel}
. We must show that
\mathscr{N}=\operatorname{Ker}Q
is a Serre subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.
Thus, let
A\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow C
be a short exact sequence in
\mathscr{C}
with
N\in\mathscr{N}
.
Applying
Q
, we get a
\mathfrak{t}
-conflation (i.e. short exact sequence as
\mathscr{D}
is abelian)
QA\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow QC
.
Thus,
QA\cong 0\cong QC
and hence
A,B\in\operatorname{Ker}Q=\mathscr{N}
. Proceeding as in the triangulated case, we see that
Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
is (isomorphic to) the Serre quotient functor of
\mathscr{C}
by
\mathscr{N}
.

The converses follow from Example
4.16
.
∎

4.4.
The Grothendieck group

Lastly in this section, we recall the definition of the Grothendieck group associated to an extriangulated category.

Definition 4.18
.

[
ZZ21
, Sec. 4]

The
Grothendieck group
of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is the abelian group

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\text{is an $\mathfrak{s}$-triangle}\,}.

Note that if
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is the extriangulated category described in Example
4.3
corresponding to a skeletally small exact category
(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E})
, then the group
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
coincides with the classical definition of the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E})
defined for an exact category. Similarly for a triangulated category.

Taking the Grothendieck groups of extriangulated categories provides a functor
K_{0}\colon\mathsf{Extri}\to\mathsf{Ab}
.
To help understand the localization process, it is useful to investigate how exact sequences in
\mathsf{Extri}
behave under the functor
K_{0}
.
In this direction, Enomoto–Saito
[
ES22
, Cor. 4.32]
proved that a certain exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
induces an exact sequence of Grothendieck groups, generalizing the results for Serre and Verdier quotients (see
[
Bas68
, Ch. VIII, Cor. 5.5]
and
[
Gro77
, VIII, Prop. 3.1]
, respectively).
We will apply Corollary
3.7
to an extriangulated localization sequence and recover
[
ES22
, Cor. 4.32]
in Corollary
5.14
.

5.
Weak Waldhausen extriangulated categories

In this section, we combine the frameworks of weak Waldhausen and of extriangulated categories. We will see
that extriangulated categories give rise to a canonical weak biWaldhausen structure, and
that an extriangulated localization sequence induces a localization sequence of weak Waldhausen categories which yields a right exact sequence of Grothendieck groups.

5.1.
Definitions and examples

We begin by showing each extriangulated category has a canonical weak biWaldhausen structure.

Example 5.1
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category.
Define

\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathfrak{s}}
to be the class of all
\mathfrak{s}
-conflations, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathfrak{s}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{C}
.
Then
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathfrak{s}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is a weak biWaldhausen category, where the cofibrations are all
\mathfrak{s}
-inflations and the fibrations are all
\mathfrak{s}
-deflations.
Since the definition of an extriangulated category is self-dual (see
[
NP19
, Caution 2.20]
), it suffices to show that
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathfrak{s}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is weak Waldhausen.

Let us check the axioms laid out in Definition
2.13
.
First, note that
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathfrak{s}}}
is closed under compositions by
(ET4)
and contains the isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}
as
\mathfrak{s}
is an additive realization (see
[
NP19
, Def. 2.10]
). Thus,
(1)(WC0)
holds.
Axiom
(1)(WC1)
follows from
\mathfrak{s}
being an additive realization,
[
NP19
, Prop. 3.7]
and
[
NP19
, Cor. 3.12]
.
Axiom
(1)(WC2)
follows from Lemma
4.2
(1)
.
It is clear that
(1)(WW0)
is satisfied.
Lastly, the Gluing Axiom
(1)(WW1)
is straightforward to verify using
[
NP19
, Cor. 3.6]
.

In addition, we observe that
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathfrak{s}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathfrak{s}})=K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

It is classical that the full subcategory of cofibrant objects in a closed model category forms a Waldhausen category; see
e.g.
[
GJ09
, Prop. II.8.1, Lem. II.8.8]
.
Example
5.1
immediately implies the following analogue.

Example 5.2
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category and suppose
\mathscr{M}=(\emph{Fib},\emph{Cof},\mathbb{W})
is an admissible model structure on
\mathscr{C}
in the sense of
[
NP19
, Def. 5.5]
. Let
\mathcal{U}
denote the full subcategory of
cofibrant
objects in
\mathscr{C}
, i.e.
X\in\mathcal{U}
if and only if the morphism
0\to X
is in
\emph{Cof}
.
Then, by
[
NP19
, Prop. 5.6]
, the subcategory
\mathcal{U}
is part of a cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{U}^{\perp_{1}})
(see
[
NP19
, Def. 4.1]
).
In particular,
\mathcal{U}
is extension-closed in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
by
[
NP19
, Rem. 4.6]
. Hence,
\mathcal{U}
inherits extriangulated, and thus also a weak biWaldhausen, structure from
\mathscr{C}
.

Example
5.1
motivates the following terminology.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category, and suppose
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a weak Waldhausen category (not necessarily the canonical one).
If any distinguished sequence in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation, then we call
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
a
weak Waldhausen extriangulated category with respect to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
(a
wWET category
, for short).
We say
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
a
weak coWaldhausen extriangulated category with respect to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
(or a
wcoWET category
) if

(\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\mathsf{op}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\mathsf{op}})
is a wWET category with respect to
(\mathscr{C}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathfrak{s}^{\mathsf{op}})
.
A
weak biWaldhausen extriangulated category with respect to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
(or a
wbiWET category
) is a triplet
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
that is a wWET and a wcoWET category with respect to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

Example 5.4
.

By Example
5.1
, there is a canonical wbiWET category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathfrak{s}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathfrak{s}})
associated to an extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

The next example is of a Waldhausen category that is not wWET (with respect to an extriangulated structure arising naturally in its context).

Example 5.5
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a skeletally small abelian category with at least one non-monomorphism (e.g.
\mathscr{A}
has a non-zero object).
As in Example
2.26
, there is a weak Waldhausen category

(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
where
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
is the class of all right exact sequences in
\mathscr{A}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{Iso}}\mathscr{C}
.

Let
(\mathscr{A},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
denote the extriangulated structure on
\mathscr{A}
induced by its abelian structure; see Example
4.3
.
Since an arbitrary right exact sequence is not a short exact sequence in general, the weak Waldhausen category
(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is not a wWET category with respect to
(\mathscr{A},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

In the above example, if we had assumed
\mathscr{A}
had a morphism that was not a weak kernel of any morphism, then it would follow that
(\mathscr{A},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is not wWET with respect to any extriangulated structure on
\mathscr{A}
.

The last example we give is weak Waldhausen but neither Waldhausen nor extension-closed in its ambient triangulated category.

Example 5.6
.

Consider the path algebra of the quiver
1\to 2\to 3
over a field, its module category and the bounded derived category of that. Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
denote this triangulated category and let
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
be the corresponding weak Waldhausen structure (see Example
5.1
).
Consider the full, additive subcategory
\mathscr{D}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{add}}\{2,3,\begin{subarray}{c}2\\
3\end{subarray},\begin{subarray}{c}1\\
2\end{subarray}\}
.
This is not extension-closed in
\mathscr{C}
as, e.g.,
\begin{subarray}{c}1\\
2\\
3\end{subarray}
is not in
\mathscr{D}
.
Define
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathscr{D}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}\cap\mathscr{D}^{\to\to}
(i.e. all
\mathfrak{s}
-conflations with all three terms lying in
\mathscr{D}
).
Define
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{\mathscr{D}}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}\cap\mathscr{D}^{\to}
(i.e. all the isomorphisms of
\mathscr{D}
).
Notice that
(1)(WC1)
holds.

Note that this does not imply
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathscr{D}}
coincides with
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\cap\mathscr{D}^{\to}
. In fact,
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathscr{D}}
is much smaller:
up to isomorphism, the only indecomposable cofibrations are
a\colon 3\to\begin{subarray}{c}2\\
3\end{subarray}
and split monomorphisms with indecomposable domain, and well-defined compositions of these.
Notice that
(1)(WC0)
holds.

In particular,
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathscr{D}}
consists only of the following
\mathfrak{s}
-conflations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3\oplus 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}2\\
3\end{subarray}\oplus X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\oplus X,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\oplus 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\oplus\mathsf{id}_{X}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3\oplus X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}2\\
3\end{subarray}\oplus X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\oplus 0,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\oplus\mathsf{id}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\oplus 0}

and split sequences with terms in
\mathscr{D}
.
Now, if
A\to B\to D
is in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathscr{D}}
and
A\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}C
is a morphism in
\mathscr{D}
, then we can take a weak pushout in
\mathscr{C}
to obtain an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation of the form
C\to E\to D
, as well as another
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation
A\to B\oplus C\to E
.
In order to show
(1)(WC2)
, we need to show that
E\in\mathscr{D}
.

•

If
A\to B\to D
is split, then
C\to E\to D
splits and
E\cong C\oplus D\in\mathscr{D}
.

•

Suppose
A\to B\to D
is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}2\\
3\end{subarray}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

We have the following cases for
c
:

–

c
is a section;

–

c
is a retraction, so either
c
is either
\mathsf{id}_{3}\colon 3\to 3
or the zero morphism
3\to 0
; or

–

c=a
.

In all cases,
C\to E\to D
splits and
E\in\mathscr{D}
.

•

More general situations will lead to direct sums of the above and so
E\in\mathscr{D}
again.

Lastly,
(1)(WW0)
and
(1)(WW1)
hold because they hold for
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.
Thus, the triplet
(\mathscr{D},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathscr{D}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{\mathscr{D}})
is weak Waldhausen. Moreover, the weak Waldhausen structure on
\mathscr{D}
is non-trivial, i.e. not just the split exact structure. The Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\mathscr{D},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathscr{D}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}_{\mathscr{D}})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.

5.2.
Theorem
4.14
induces a localization sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}

For the rest of this section, we work under the following setup so Theorem
4.14
applies.

Setup 5.7
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a skeletally small extriangulated category with corresponding weak biWaldhausen category
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})=(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}_{\mathfrak{s}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
Suppose
(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})
is a thick subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, and that
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}\coloneqq\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is saturated and
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}\subseteq(\mathscr{C}/[\mathscr{N}])^{\to}
satisfies conditions
(MR1)–(MR4) of
[
NOS22
, p. 343]
.

In particular, Setup
5.7
implies there is an exact sequence (
4.2
) in
\mathsf{Extri}
by Theorem
4.14
.

Lemma 5.8
.

The triplet
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a wbiWET category with respect to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

Proof.

Note that
(1)(WC0)
–
(1)(WC2)
hold for
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
since they already hold for
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})
.
Condition
(1)(WW0)
is clear.
To show the Gluing Axiom
(1)(WW1)
, consider the diagram (
2.3
), where
x,y,z
all lie in
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
. Using Lemma
4.2
and (ET
3
), we obtain a morphism

(5.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}\oplus C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}\oplus C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
-c_{1}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b_{1},\amph g_{1}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\oplus z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}f_{2}\\
-c_{2}\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}b_{2},\amph g_{2}\end{pmatrix}}

of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles.
Note that

y\oplus z\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}

by
(M0)
.
Applying
Q
, we have that
Qx
and

Q(y\oplus z)

are isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
.
As
\mu\colon\mathbb{E}\Rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}(Q-,Q-)
is natural, we have that
(Qx,Q(y\oplus z),Qw)
is a morphism of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
-triangles.
Hence, by
[
NP19
, Cor. 3.6]
, we know
Qw
is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
, and hence

w\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
as
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is saturated.
Therefore,
(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
is a wWET category with respect to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
. In fact, the above can be dualised and so we are done.
∎

Thus, using Lemma
2.20
and Remark
2.21
, there is a sequence

(5.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}}

of wbiWET categories (with respect to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
) and the assumptions of Setup
3.1
are met.
We may now compare Definitions
4.13
and
3.2
.

Lemma 5.9
.

We have
\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}=\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}=\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

The containment
\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
follows from Lemma
3.3
. Conversely, if

A\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\twoheadrightarrow C

is an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation (i.e. lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}
) with
C\in\mathscr{N}
, applying the exact functor
(Q,\mu)
of extriangulated categories yields
the
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
-conflation

QA\overset{Qf}{\rightarrowtail}QB\twoheadrightarrow 0
.
It follows that
Qf
is an isomorphism, so
f\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
as
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is saturated. Thus,
f\in\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}
.

The equality
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}
can be shown in a similar way thanks to Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Example 5.10
.

As a special case of Lemma
5.9
, if
\mathscr{C}
is a triangulated category with a thick subcategory
\mathscr{N}
, then we have
\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}=\mathcal{L}=\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}=\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}
by Example
4.16
(1)
.

We now show that the exact sequences in
\mathsf{Extri}
coming from Theorem
4.14
(which include Verdier and Serre quotients, see Example
4.16
) give rise to localization sequences (see Definition
2.22
) in
\mathsf{wWald}
and induce right exact sequences of abelian groups under
K_{0}
.

Proposition 5.11
.

The sequence (
5.2
) is a localization sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}

and

(5.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{inc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}})}

is a right exact sequence of their Grothendieck groups.

Proof.

We have already seen above that (
5.2
) is a sequence of weak biWaldhausen categories.
From Definition
4.13
and the discussion immediately following it, we know a morphism in
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is a finite composition of morphisms from
\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{R}
.
By Lemma
5.9
, this implies

\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
consists of morphisms that are finite compositions of morphisms from

\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ac}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{ac}}\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}
.
Hence, Corollary
3.7
applies and we are done.
∎

An example of a localization sequence in
\mathsf{wWald}
that does not sit in
\mathsf{Extri}
in general is given in Theorem
6.14
.

Lemma 5.12
.

We have
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}=\mathscr{N}
and
(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})

is the weak biWaldhausen category corresponding to the extriangulated category

(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle X\in\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}

\displaystyle\iff 0\to X\text{ lies in }\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}=\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}

by definition of
\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}

\displaystyle\iff Q(0\to X)\text{ is an isomorphism}

as
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}
is saturated

\displaystyle\iff X\in\operatorname{Ker}Q

see (
1.2
)

\displaystyle\iff X\in\mathscr{N}

by Theorem
4.14
,

and hence
\mathscr{N}=\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}
.
Furthermore, since

\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})^{\to\to}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}\cap\mathscr{N}^{\to\to}

is just the collection of
\mathfrak{s}
-conflations with all terms in
\mathscr{N}
, the second assertion follows.
∎

5.3.
Theorem
4.14
induces a right exact sequence in
\mathsf{Ab}

Recall that we defined the homotopy category of a weak Waldhausen category in Section
3.1

(see Definition
3.11
).
Such homotopy categories arise in extriangulated localizations as we now explain.

Example 5.13
.

Let (
4.2
) be an extriangulated localization sequence in the sense of Theorem
4.14
.
In particular, there is an extriangulated localization functor

(Q,\mu)\colon(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})\to(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
.
Setting
\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime}
to be the class of all
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
-conflations and
\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}
to be the class of isomorphisms in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
,
we have that the triplet

(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
is the wbiWET category corresponding to
(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})

(see Examples
5.1
and
5.4
).
Moreover, as discussed earlier in this section (see Proposition
5.11
), we have the localization sequence
(
5.2
)
in
\mathsf{wWald}
,
and it follows from
[
NOS22
, Lem. 3.32]
that
(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime})
is the weak Waldhausen homotopy category

\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})
.
Lastly, we note that the localization functor
Q
is an exact functor

(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})

of wbiWET categories.

We can now combine some of our key results to see that an extriangulated localization sequence induces a right exact sequence of Grothendieck groups, and in particular recover
[
ES22
, Cor. 4.32]
with a new proof.
We remark that the hypotheses we are currently assuming are equivalent to those assumed in
[
ES22
, Cor. 4.32]
, which are
[
ES22
, Cond. 4.4]
and that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
is saturated.

Corollary 5.14

(Enomoto–Saito’s Extriangulated Localization Theorem)
.

The extriangulated localization sequence
(
4.2
) of Theorem
4.14

induces the following right exact sequence in
\mathsf{Ab}
.

(5.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{inc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(Q)}

Proof.

Proposition
5.11
tells us that (
5.3
) is exact.
We have the equalities:

\displaystyle K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})

\displaystyle=K_{0}(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})

by Lemma
5.12
and Example
5.1
,

\displaystyle K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})

\displaystyle=K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})

by Example
5.1
, and

\displaystyle K_{0}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}))

\displaystyle=K_{0}(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})

by Example
5.13
and Example
5.1
.

Note that Setup
3.10
is satisfied, so by Proposition
3.12
there is the isomorphism

K_{0}(Q)\colon K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}K_{0}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ho}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})).

Therefore, (
5.4
) is exact.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{W}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{\mathsf{Seq}},\operatorname{\mathsf{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{inc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mathsf{inc})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(Q)}

∎

6.
Application to the index in triangulated categories

In this section, we specialize to the case when
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is a skeletally small, idempotent complete triangulated category with suspension functor
[1]
.
The index with respect to an
n
-cluster tilting subcategory
\mathscr{X}
of
\mathscr{C}
was introduced in
[
Jør21
]
, and it induces an isomorphism between
K_{0}^{\mathsf{sp}}(\mathscr{X})
and
K_{0}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{R}_{\mathscr{X}^{\perp_{0}}},\mathfrak{s}^{R}_{\mathscr{X}^{\perp_{0}}})
, the Grothendieck group of a
relative
extriangulated structure of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
; see Definition
6.1
, Proposition
6.3
, Corollary
6.6
.
This isomorphism was proved in this generality in
[
OS23
]
, where it was called the
JS index isomorphism
,
but it first manifested as
[
PPPP23
, Prop. 4.11]
,
which has led to the index being defined with respect to more general subcategories

[
JS24b
,
FJS24b
,
FJS24a
]
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer.
A subcategory
\mathscr{X}
of a triangulated category
\mathscr{C}
is called
n
-cluster tilting
if
\mathscr{X}
is functorially finite and

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}

\displaystyle=\Set{C\in\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{X},C[i])=0\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n-1}

\displaystyle=\Set{C\in\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{C}(C,\mathscr{X}[i])=0\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n-1}.

If
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an extriangulated category and
\mathbb{F}\subseteq\mathbb{E}
is an additive subfunctor (see
[
HLN21
, Def. 3.7]
) such that
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}})
is an extriangulated category, then we say

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}})
is
relative
to or a
relative theory
of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.
The identity
\mathsf{id}_{\mathscr{C}}
and inclusion
\mathbb{F}\subseteq\mathbb{E}
constitute an exact functor
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}})\to(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
, witnessing that

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}})
is an extriangulated subcategory of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
in the sense of
[
Hau21
, Def. 3.7]

(cf.
[
JS24a
, Thm. 2.12]
).
We need the following before we recall the specific relative structure we use.

Definition 6.2
.

If
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}
are subcategories of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
,
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U})
denotes the full subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
consisting of objects
X
for which there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation
V\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow X
with
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
V\in\mathcal{V}
.
The full subcategory
\operatorname{\mathsf{CoCone}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is defined similarly.

If
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is a triangulated category, then it follows that

\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U})
if and only if
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{\mathsf{CoCone}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U})
since we can rotate triangles in
\mathscr{C}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is a triangulated category with an extension-closed subcategory
\mathscr{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
that is closed under direct summands and satisfies
\operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(\mathscr{N},\mathscr{N})=\mathscr{C}
.
Then there are extriangulated categories

\mathscr{C}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}\coloneqq(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}),\quad\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}\coloneqq(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}),\quad\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}\coloneqq(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}),

all relative to
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
,
where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}\cap\mathbb{E}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}(C,A)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Set{h\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)}{\forall x\colon N\to C\text{ with }N\in\mathscr{N}\text{, we have }hx\in[\,\mathscr{N}[1]\,]}

\displaystyle\,=\Set{h\colon C\to A[1]}{h[-1]\textnormal{\ factors through an object in\ }\mathscr{N}},

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}(C,A)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Set{h\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)}{\forall y\colon A\to N\text{ with }N\in\mathscr{N}\text{, we have }y\circ h[-1]\in[\,\mathscr{N}[-1]\,]}

(6.2)

\displaystyle\,=\Set{h\colon C\to A[1]}{h\textnormal{\ factors through an object in\ }\mathscr{N}}.

Proof.

That
\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}=(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}})
, as defined in (
6.1
), is an extriangulated category follows from
[
Oga22b
, Prop. 2.1]
.
The equality (
6.2
) is
[
OS23
, Lem. 3.14]
.
The assertions concerning
\mathscr{C}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}
follow similarly.
It also follows that
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}
is an extriangulated category.
∎

We need one last definition before we can recall Corollary
6.6
.

Definition 6.4
.

[
OS23
, Def. 4.2]

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an extriangulated category with a subcategory
\mathscr{X}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
.
The top row of

(6.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n-2}g_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}g_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

is said to be a
finite
\mathscr{X}
-resolution
of
C\in\mathscr{C}
if

C_{i+1}\overset{f_{i}}{\longrightarrow}X_{i}\overset{g_{i}}{\longrightarrow}C_{i}

is an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation
for each
0\leq i\leq n-1

with
X_{i}\in\mathscr{X}

for all
0\leq i\leq n
.

Example 6.5
.

[
OS23
, Exam. 4.3]

Suppose
\mathscr{C}
is an idempotent complete triangulated category and that
\mathscr{X}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is
n
-cluster tilting. Then each object
C\in\mathscr{C}
admits a finite
\mathscr{X}
-resolution

X_{n-1}\to\cdots\to X_{1}\to X_{0}\to C

of length at most
n-1
.
In fact, this
\mathscr{X}
-resolution is an
\mathscr{X}
-resolution in the relative extriangulated
\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}
where

\mathscr{N}\coloneqq\mathscr{X}^{\perp_{0}}\coloneqq\Set{C\in\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{X},C)=0}
.

The statement of Corollary
6.6
below differs slightly from the statement of
[
OS23
, Cor. 5.5]
: an extriangulated structure
\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{X}}_{R}
is used in
[
OS23
, Cor. 5.5]
, but we have
\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{X}}_{R}=\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}
by
[
OS23
, Lem. 3.15]
where
\mathscr{N}\coloneqq\mathscr{X}^{\perp_{0}}
.
Furthermore, special cases of this result have been shown in
[
PPPP23
, Prop. 4.11]
and
[
JS24b
, Thm. 4.10]
.

Corollary 6.6

(JS index isomorphism)
.

[
OS23
, Cor. 5.5]

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a skeletally small, idempotent complete triangulated category,

\mathscr{X}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
an
n
-cluster tilting subcategory, and

\mathscr{N}\coloneqq\mathscr{X}^{\perp_{0}}
.
Then there exists an isomorphism

(6.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{ind}}_{\mathscr{X}}\colon K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}})

\displaystyle\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}K_{0}^{\mathsf{sp}}(\mathscr{X})

\displaystyle[C]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{ind}}_{\mathscr{X}}(C)\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}[X_{i}]

\displaystyle[X]

\displaystyle\longmapsfrom[X],

where

X_{n-1}\to\cdots\to X_{1}\to X_{0}\to C

is a finite
\mathscr{X}
-resolution of
C
in
\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}
.

The element
\operatorname{\mathsf{ind}}_{\mathscr{X}}(C)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}[X_{i}]
in
K_{0}^{\mathsf{sp}}(\mathscr{X})
is the
triangulated index of
C
with respect to
\mathscr{X}
(see
[
Jør21
, Def. 3.3]
), and is a generalization of the index Palu defined in
[
Pal08
, Def. 2.1]
for
2
-cluster tilting subcategories.
Our goal in this section is to understand and give a different proof of Corollary
6.6
via our Weak Waldhausen Localization Theorem
3.6
.

In the remainder of Section
6
, we assume the following.

Setup 6.7
.

Suppose
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
corresponds to a skeletally small, idempotent complete, triangulated category with suspension
[1]
.
Let
\mathscr{N}
denote an extension-closed
subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, which is closed under direct summands and such that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(\mathscr{N},\mathscr{N})=\mathscr{C}
.

6.1.
An abelian localization of a triangulated category

In this subsection we study further the relative extriangulated categories defined in Proposition
6.3
. In particular, in Theorem
6.14
we will see that the groups

K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}})
,
K_{0}(\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}})
and
K_{0}(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})
are all isomorphic.

Remark 6.8
.

We record some facts on the extriangulated structures inherited by
\mathscr{N}
.

(1)

Since
\mathscr{N}
is extension-closed in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
, it is still extension-closed in the relative extriangulated subcategories
\mathscr{C}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}
,
\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}
.

(2)

We have equalities

(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})=(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}}^{R}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{R}|_{\mathscr{N}})=(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}}^{L}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{L}|_{\mathscr{N}})=(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}|_{\mathscr{N}})

as extriangulated categories.
Indeed, if
N_{0}\longrightarrow N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{2}
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation whose terms
N_{i}
all lie in
\mathscr{N}
, then it is clearly an
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}
-conflation given the descriptions of
\mathbb{E}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}
and
\mathbb{E}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}
in Proposition
6.3
.

With respect to the relative structure
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}
, the pair
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{N})
yields a saturated class
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\to}
of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
so that
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}
satisfies the needed conditions (MR1)–(MR4) to obtain an exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
.
Although
\mathscr{N}
may not be thick in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
in general, passing to
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}
allows us to take an extriangulated localization in the sense of Section
4.3
.
Theorem
6.10
summarises some key results from
[
Oga22b
]
that we use. We need a definition in preparation.

Definition 6.9
.

[
Oga21
, Def. 2.7]

An additive functor
F\colon(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{t})\to\mathscr{A}
from an extriangulated category
(\mathscr{D},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{t})
to an abelian category
\mathscr{A}
is called
right exact
if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fg}

is exact in
\mathscr{A}
whenever

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is a
\mathfrak{t}
-conflation.

Left exact
functors are defined dually.

Theorem 6.10
.

We have the following.

(1)

\mathscr{N}
is closed under taking cones of
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{R}
-inflations and cocones of
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{L}
-deflations. That is, if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is an
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{R}
-conflation and
A,B\in\mathscr{N}
, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{Cone}}(f)\in\mathscr{N}
; closure under cocones of
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{L}
-deflations is defined similarly.

(2)

\mathscr{N}
is thick subcategory of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}
, and
the corresponding class
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\to}
defined in Definition
4.13
is saturated and
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}}
satisfies (MR1)–(MR4) of
[
NOS22
, p. 343]
.
In addition, we have

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{ret}}\circ\mathcal{L}=\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{sec}}
.

(3)

There is an extriangulated localization
(Q,\mu)\colon\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}\to(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}})
of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}
with respect to
\mathscr{N}
,
and it gives rise to the following exact sequence in
\mathsf{Extri}
.

(6.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{N},\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{N}},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathscr{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{inc},\iota)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\mu)}

(4)

Moreover, the extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{N}}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}})
corresponds to an abelian category
and

Q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}

is a cohomological functor.

(5)

The functor
Q\colon\mathscr{C}^{R}_{\mathscr{N}}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
(resp.
Q\colon\mathscr{C}^{L}_{\mathscr{N}}\to\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
) is right exact (resp. left exact).

Proof.

For
(1)
and
(2)
, see
[
OS23
, Thm. 3.12(1),(2)]
.
Part
(3)
follows from Theorem
4.14
given
(2)
(see also
[
OS23
, Thm. 3.12(3)]
).
Part
(4)
follows from
[
OS23
, Thm. 3.16]
and
(5)
is
[
Oga22b
, Cor. 4.4]
.
∎

Let us give a useful characterization of the morphisms in
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
obtained in Theorem
6.10
(2)
, by describing the morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
that become epic (resp. monic) in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
via
Q
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

be a triangle in
\mathscr{C}
.
Then the statements
(1)
–
(3)
below are equivalent.

(1)

Qg
is epic in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
.

(2)

g
is an
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{R}
-deflation and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is an
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{R}
-triangle.

(3)

h
factors through an object in
\mathscr{N}
.

Dually, the statements
(1’)
–
(3’)
below equivalent.

(1’)

Qf
is monic in
\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{N}
.

(2’)

f
is an
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{L}
-inflation and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is an
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathscr{N}}^{L}
-triangle.

(3’)

h[-1]
factors through an object in
\mathscr{N}
.

In particular,
g\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{N}}
if and only if both
f
and
h
factor through objects in
\mathscr{N}
.

Proof.

The equivalence
\ref{Qg-epic}\Leftrightarrow\ref{h-factors}

follows from
[
Oga22b
, Lem. 2.6]
,
and

\ref{g-defl}\Leftrightarrow\ref{h-factors}
is just Proposition
6.3
.
The proofs of the equivalences
